INFIRMARY BAZAAR.

CLOSING DAY'S PROCEEDINGS

Gratifying Result.

The financial result of the great bazaar and fete in aid of the Infirmary which came to a close on Saturday must be exceedingly gortifying to those who have worked so arduously for its success. Notwithstanding that the weather was far from being all that could be desired for Buell an event, the bazaar and its numerous attractions vere so well patronized that a sum of over £2,600 was raised. After paying expenses there will be about 2,000 guineas to hand over to the extension fund. The bazaar was re-opened in brilliant sunshine on Saturday afternoon by Mrs. Langford-Brooke, of Mere Hall. There was a large attendance, Mr. Henry Greenall presiding. The CHAIRMAN announced the previous days' pro- ceeds as follows :—Wednesday, £6BO 7s. 7d.; Thursday. £413 19s. 3d.; Friday, .9.465 4s. 2d. It was necessary to raise at least £6OO that day and he was perfectly certain that if they continued to work as they had done, they would succeed in their object. (Applause.) It was a pity that they had such a wet day on Thursday; but altogether they had been very fortunate in the weather. Last week people told him that the bazaar would be a fearful fisaco and suggested that they should pray for fine weather. He quoted the words of au eminent gentleman who said: "It is not the slightest use praying for fine weather unless the wind changes. You might as well pray for green gooseberries at Christmas.' (Laughter.) He was glad to say that the wind had changed. (Applause.) Another reason they had ha a fine weather was that they had had the smiling face of the Mayor there, and he always added charm to the proceedings. (Applause). Mr. Greenall proceeded to commend splendid work of the stallholders, and said nothing could have been more harmonious than the working of the various committees. Special thanks were due to the promoters of the enter- tainments, and the work of those who had managed the fair-ground attractions had been extraordinary succcessful. in a bazaar like that rich and poor did their best, and one of the most charming things in con- nection with the event was that a large number of mem- bers of Stockton Heath mothers' meeting volunteered, as they could not afford to give anything to the Infirmary—an institution in which they were keenly interested—to give their services at the card. (Applause.) Mr. Greenall concluded by asking Mrs. Langford-Brooke to declare the bazaar open. Mrs. LANGFORD-BROOKE thanked the promoters very sincerely for the honour they had done her in asking her to perform the ceremony. She was quite am e'they bad had the object of the bazaar ably put before them during the past few days, but 'she thought! there was only one word required to draw forth the generosity which was so sadly needed, and that was the word "infirmary." She felt certain that if any of the buyers or sellers who were present that day in the midst of that gay scene were able to give one glance into the future, and as a result to see one single smile on a poor, weary suffering face and feel that it was partly through their endeavours that that smile had been brought there, they would quickly renew their efforts. Mr.and Mrs. Greenall deserved the great gratitude of all concerned in the bazaar (applause) for placing the grounds at their dis- posal. She cordially thanked them again for the privilege they had done her in allowing her to declare the bazaar open. (Applause). Captain CROSFIELD in moving a hearty vote of thanks to Mrs. Langford-Brooke, assured her that she had come amongst a very happy family of workers who bad thrown themselves with energy and earnestness into the work of the bazaar and into the task of enticing sovereigns out of fat purses and shillings out of lean ones. (Laughter). They were doubly grateful to Mrs. Langford-Brooke for coming to stimuhrte the workers after their three days' arduous toil. There was an old German proverb to the effecc that, sorrow which was shared was thereby halved ; joy which was shared was thereby doubled. Mrs. Langford-Brooke's presence increased the joy of thu workers in that effort to pro- vide a home for the sick and wounded. (Appl6use.) Mr. F. W. MONKS seconded and the vote was heartily accorded. A pleasing little ceremony then took place, Mrs. Ralph Johnson introducing Miss Kate Davenport and Miss Lattice Warburton, who presented ths sum of £8 is. 6d. subscribed by the Secondary School pupils. They in their turn were recipients of handsome boxes as a recognition of tkeir efforts in collecting the highest amounts. The Rev. Father Witerms, said they ought not to close the proceedings without according horn their hearts a vote of thanks to Mr. and Mrs. Greenill for placing that beautiful park at their disposal. (Applause.) What the state of the park would be on Monday he did not know. (Laughter.) It would be a sad sight from Mr. and Mrs.Greensll's beautiful house,but he was sure it would gladden their hearts if the bazaar had brought an abundance of money for the extension of the Infirmary. (Applause.) The vote was carried with hearty cheers. Mr. GREENALL in a humorous reply said he was an absolute Conservative, but in these things he was under home rule. (Laughter.) He was practically not con- sulted in the matter at all. He was told his wife had arranged that the fête and bazaar should be held there, and he said : "What infernal rot ! It will spoil the whole place." (Laughter.) However, he was not taken the slightest notice of. (Lauehter). If by allow- ing the people to come there they had done anything to assist in the enlargement of the Infirmary, that was all the thanks they wanted. (Applause). During the afternoon, with the exception of a shower the weather was beautifully fine and there was a con- stant stream of visitors to the grounds. In the evening rain fell heavily, but nevertheless a good business was done, and the bazaar was a splendid success.

WOMF.N'S CHAT.

The Kaiser as an Artist.—'T.itere aro few things which the versatile German Emperor cannot do, but there has been some hesitation in taking him seriously as an artist and art critic. Any doubts on that point however will be removed by Professor Boese's book on the subject, which goes to show that the Kaiser has inherited all these arttstic tastes and talents of his mother. It reproduces is number of his Majesty's drawings, relating to all kinds of subjects, and these are sufficient to prove the Emperor's knowledge and skill, whether it is the planning and drawing of a new battleship, cathedral, or some allegorical picture. In painting and sculpture the late Empress Frederick was the most talented of Queen Victoria's children. Many of her works were exhibited at the Paris Salon and the Berlin Academy, and evidently the Kaiser is to .he congratulated on his inheritance of her artistic accomplishments. Railway Travelling in Scotland.—One does not 'often meet with an embarrassing amount of courtesy from Scottish railway officials, but one thing that always strikes the visitor to bcotland,is the cheap and excellent facilities it affords to railway travellers. The system of issuing circular tourist tickets is an immense convenience to holiday makers, whilst the long-distance day excursion trains are marvels of cheapness. Those who like plenty of railway travelling or their money, ought to be satisfied with a journey Out and home of 400 or 500 miles for 4s. or 55., and such trips are available from Glasgow and Edinburgh, and many of the principal cities. People who have travelled in all parts of the world, have been heard to say that they know nothing of anything quite so cheap and excellent its the train and boat excursions from Glasgow to the lochs, and the Firth of Clyde. Certainly there is nothing in the south of Britain to compare with this, although one would think the successful Scottish system of recognizing that the interests of the railways and the public are identical, was well worth imitating. A Custom of Etiquette.—An Austrian Count recently started a crusade to abolish the masculine custom of hat-raising, but, so far, the movement has not met with much success. On the Continent, a gentleman has not only to raise ls hat, on meeting a lady, but cannot put it on again unEl she gives him her permission. We are not so exacting, but in etiquette,it is a case of different countries, different matters. Over here the gentleman has to wait for the lady's recognition, before raising his hat, but in Germany the lady would be very much offended at any such hesitation. It is urged by the reformers that the military salute should ho substituted for the present custom. this tnieht be better under- stood on the continent but we are not a military people and it will be usually noticed that the man who spends his holiday at the seaside without any bat, generally recognises his lady friends with only the familiar little nod. _ _ _

The Voice.—Among other charms which women are supposed to have sacrificed since they took to riding the bicycle,and left the over-burdened home to earn their living, is the loss of the soft low voice and gentle manners of our mothers and grandmothers. Perhaps it is because of the more noisy times in which we live, although some are disposed to attribute the thunderous manner in which we speak to one another, to the modern vulgar habit of wishing to attract attention. This is a favourite point with public writers who profess to worship the old ideal of wonfanly charms, but then there are always critics who think that the manners and customs and everything else of the present day, are not nearly so good as they used to be. Formerly robust health and vigour were looked upon as vulgar in women, but this, like many other old ideas, is a little out of date nowadays. Still there is no doubt some need for us to be on our guard against falling into the rough voice and harsh laugh of which our critics com- plain. American womea suffer in this way, more than we do, and no German, and very few German women. were ever heard to talk in a voice soft and low. At the same time the cultured voice is a desideratum we should not allow athletic and sporting attraetions, or the business occupations to lead us wholly to neglect. An Old Wedding Rieg Belief, and Wife Selling.— Applications in the Police Courts show that many of the old hazy notions regarding marriage still prevail amongst people of the humbler classes. Recently a woman applied to a London magistrate to be relieved of her matrimonial burden on the score that her wedding ring was of brass, and experience at the Police Courts showes that such applications are by n& means a very exceptional circumstance. This belief that a marriage was not binding, if the wedding ring was not of pure gold, used to be a very conkmon Offie, but then in the old days, many extraordinary notions were entertained regarding marriage, and among them the supposed right of a man to sell his wife in the Market place, with a halter ronnd her neck. According to the ideas and customs of the time it was not considernd a legal sale unless the woman was delivered to her, purchaser with a halter round her neck. It may be that such occurrences were never regarded seriously, but the view taken of them among the ignorant classes were quite sufficient to justify the old French gibe on the subject. The July Sales.—The time has arrived for us to make out our list of requirements for the July Sales, which so shortly now will be in full swing. Owing to the exceptionally cold, late spring and early summer, the "bargains" promise to be phenomenal. and those girls and women who habitually wait for the sales before replenishing their summer wardrobe, will speedily dis- cover that the waiting has been more than worth while this year. Muslin and all other delicate washing dresses, will be greatly reduced, so also will cotton and linen gowns, and underskirts, while dainty millinery will be going for a mere song. The wise woman by next week will have gone carefully over her wardrobe, and -made notes of all purchases deemed desirable, leaving little or nothing to memory. She will also have firmly made up her, mind not to be tempted to buy things simply because they are cheap, or invest in any " bargain " that she cannot see her way to use. Airy. fairy slimmer fabrics will be considerably marked down, more so than usual, but the greatest reductions will undoubtedly be found in the ready-made departments. A Sensible Mode.—lligh dinner frocks have evidently " caueht on," that is for all save ceremonious occasions. Needless to say such gowns are fashioned of more delicate fabrics and in much smarter styles than when destined for afternoon wear, and a certain amount of transparency obtains of course obtains about the neck and arms. Still,these " creations "are very much more desirable for restaurant and theatre wear than the one time customary low cut gown. We borrowed the notion from our French sisters, and the American woman has followed our example. and is exploiting it for all she is worth. s Modish Gathers.—When the fabrics permit, skirts are being gathered at the waist, the amount of fulness needless to say, being regulated to suit the wearer. Slim individuals are wearing close gathers all round, the" more abundant "are restricting the gathers to the back and sides, while the really substantial are content- ing themselves with gathers at the back only, and a slight easing of the fabric over the hips. 'I he Getting-up of Washing Dresses.—Gum Arabic— in the proporation of about one teaspoonful to a quart of made starch—should always be used when getting up muslin, cotton voile, batiste, indeed all-light weight washing dresses intended for wear at the seaside. It will then he fornd that the sea mists which usually and very speedily reduce such toilets to limp rags, will have little or no effect upon them, and all one has to do should they become damp, is to spread them out care- fully, and place rolls of tissue paper in the sleeves and under frills and ruffles. When dry the muslins, voiles, etc., treated as described, will be stiff, and fresh look- ing, and should tbey become tumbled a warm iron will grant to them a new lease of life. MADGE.

A HINT OF VALUE.

Those who get on in the world are those who never fail to take a valuable hint when it comes their way. Here is a hint, which may prove of great value to you. "For years I was suffering from had pains in my back, and Doan's pills cured me," says Mrs. M. Jones, 133, Marshhouse-lane, Warrington. " My back was so painful when I stooped that I had to put my hands to my hips for support. The doctor threated me, butt got no relief, and when a friend advised toe to try Doan's pills, I did so. The medicine quite cured me then, and I kept well until a few weeks ago, hut I took some more of Doan's pills, and they soon put me right. (Signed) Margaret Jones." Then, nearly tire years later, when one of our repre- sentatives saw Mrs. Jones, she said :—" I have not needed to use your pills since I told of the good they had done me, but I have just started them again, as I , have taken cold in my kidneys. ; "I had been suffering off and on for three years when I used the pills before. The trouble started with a cold, which I caught when I was very much run down ; I had backache first, and then headaches and giddiness; here seemed to be specks before my eyes, and I was short-winded and always felt tired. The Examiner, Satulday, June 29, 1907

WOMAN'S REALM.

DRESS-FASHION-HOME.

WOMEN'S WORK IN WARFARE. ! Several of the great nations have been partly represented by , women delegates at the Interna- tional Red Cross Convention in London. This is eminently right and proper, as the main ob- . ject of .the Red Cross Society is precisely the woman's share in regard to warfare—to go after the soldier, and do what may be possible to re- lieve the misery of war. Of course, evqry great war has called forth abundant private charity to relieve the misery caused; but the Red Cross Society organises and prepares beforehand for ihis beneficent work, instead of leaving it to be hastily and wastefully and imperfectly under- taken in the crisis of the need. The work has long ago conquered the suffrages of the Govern- ments of all civilised nations, and the periodical Conventions are those of delegates from the actual and responsible rulers of the world. This makes it interesting that women have been ap- pointed delegates, not only by America and Eng- land, but also by France, Switzerland, and Italy, together with leading statesmen, army officers, and doctors. One of the English delegates is Miss Ethel McCaul, a nurse who was appointed by her Majesty's influence to go to the seat of war during the Russo-Japanese conflict to • study the excellent Japanese arrangements for saving life. •In America the Jsueccss of the Red Cross movement—in thebeginning—depended upon Miss Clara Barton, who took up the Red Cross idea, and organised its practical success. She was known to her own people as "The Florence Nightingale of America.

MUSLIN FROCK FOR HOT DAYS.

i A dainty dress for the warm weather is shewn in our illustration. It is a pretty model of white spotted or sprigged muslin, and is trimmed with bands of muslin insertion. The skirt is loosely pleated in at the waist, the hem being supple- mented by a full flounce. set on beneath a band of insertion. Above this is a narrower frill,

edged with Valenciennes lace, and finished with insertion. The bodice is also trimmed with bands of the muslin insertion, and is arranged with a prettily-shaped fichu, which is edged with lace, and is tucked horizontally to within two or three inches from the edge, where it is left to form a frill. The full puffed sleeves are gathered into a smaller puff at the elbow, and finished with a lace-edged frill.

THE NEWEST FASHION".

Many of the fashions this year seem strangely at variance, and it is quite difficult to reconcile them. The figure is supposed to be defined more sharply than has been fashionable the last two or three years. At the same time, clothes must not look as though fitted too tightly. There must not be any pouch in the front of the bodice, yet a loose, easy appearance must be given. A per- fect fitting lining is absolutely essential to begin with, and then on this foundation the bodice itself is draped and the trimmings are disposed on the most becoming lines. It would seem, re- marks." Pepita" in the Mark Lane Express, as though all this attention to detail were unneces- sary, but the result prove it is not, and well rev pay the trouble and time taken.

LINEN COSTUNIE FOR THE RIVER

For river or seaside wear triped linen cos- tumes are particularly smart. Our sketch shows a neat littlo costume of white linen, patterned with a tiny vieux-roe stripe. The skirt is a igia, in well-gored shape, and is trimmed with Tias straps, finishing in a point about a foot from the bottom of the skirt, each point being adorned with a large button covered with rose-coloured

linen. The short Eton coatee is trimmed with a wide strap over each shoulder, and has smart little waistcoat fronts of - white pique. adorned with groups of small pearl buttons. The belt is of rose-coloured leather or suede; and to com- plete the costume a shady Leghorn hat is worn, trimmed with a scarf of vieux-rose silk, arranged in loops. and long fringed ends, which hang over the ode of the brim.

FItOGS AS PETS.

The . most fashionable pets at the moment, writes Lady Phyllis in the Bystfinacr. are frogs. The variety chiefly in request comes, as cf course it should do. from Spain. I have not yet seen the special Spanish species. but I have had a good deal of experience of the tiny green Euro- pean tree frog. He is the jolliest little chap, equally fascinating and pretty, perfectly tame, and easy to keep—not possessing the delicacies which make foreign birds, for instance, such a problem. His food consists chiefly of flies.

TO-DAY'S RECIPE.

STRAWBERRY MERINGUE TARls.—First make a short crust, as follows: Ingredients: 6oz. of flour, 3oz. of butter, loz. of castof sugar. I egg. a pinch of salt. Put the flour through the sieve. Add the sugar and salt to it, and then rub in the butter very lightly with the finger-tips. Beat up the egg, add it to the flour. &c., and knead all together until the mixture is a smooth, firm paste. Roll out, stamp into rounds, and with these line some small patty pans. These pastry cases must be baked first without the fruit, put- ting a little uncooked rice into each to keep the paste from rising. Then take out the rice, fill the cases with picked strawberries, and cover them with fine castor sugar and a little powdered cinnamon. Beat the whites of three eggs to a stiff froth. Mix with them iloz. of castor sugar and put sonic or tins meringue on each ot the tartlets, being careful to completely hide the strawberries. Put into a moderato oven, and cook until the meringue is well sot and coloured a golden brown. Servo with whipped cream, flavoured with Tatlilla, _

PENKETH TRAGEDY.

TWO WARRINGTON MEN SUFFOCATED IN A WELL.

Fatal) Bravery.

Two Warrington nlen,Williain Payne J5l), 9,Welifield-

. . street, brickseiter, and 'George Bell (53), 110, Wellfleld- street, met with a terrible death at Penketh last Friday evening. It appears that they were engaged by Mr. Albert Payne, constructor, and brother of William Payne, to repair a pump at the Lion Farm in the occupation of Mr. Peter Boardman. For some reason the men decided te'lincover a well which had not been opened for more than fifty years. They procured a ladder and without testing the atmosphere in any way, Bell entered the well. He had gone ten teet when he was seen to fall off the ladder into the water, evidently having been overcome by poisonous gases. Payne immediately went to his comrade's assistance, but, whilst on the ladder he was also overcome, and- fell into the water. A labourer named Noon emprayed on the farm, witnessed the occurrence, and at once gave the alarm. A tanner named Woods and a Dumber of other men came on the scene followed by l'oliee Constable Houghton and Sergeant Smith. Grappling operations were at once commenced, and the bodies were recovered. Dr. Murray who had been sent for, with the assistance of several others, tried artificial respiration, but without success. The tragedy created a painful sensstion in the district. a distressing feature 'being that both men leave widows and families. THE INQUEST. 'AN ERROR OF JUDGMENT. Mr. S. Brighbuse, County Coroner, held the inquest at the Warrington Police Station on Tuesday afternoon. Mr. E. A. Connor represented the families of the deceased ; Mr. Steel appeared on behalf of Mr. Payne, contractor, by whom the deceased were employed; and Mr. Willett represented "other interests." Thomas Bell, general labourer, 110, Wellfield-street, said George Bell was his father, and he had resided with him. Witness lust saw his father alive on Thurs-

day, when he was in his usual health. Deceased was brought home on Suaday. Witness's father worked for Messrs. Monks, Hall and Co., but had been employed by Mr. Payne, contractor, to do certain work at Lion Farm. Albert Payne, joiner, 86,Bramball-street,said William Payne was his father, and was employed by Messrs. J. Payne and Co., builders and contractors. Witness last saw him alive at 5.30 'p.m. on Friday. He saw his dead body on Sunday. John Noon, farm labourer, Lion Farm, stated that Mr. Albert Payne, 122, Hood-lane Sankey Bridges, had been engaged doing repairs at the Wm. On Friday, George Bell and William Payne came about six o'clock to repair the pump. Payne got a ladder and placed it in the well, and without testing the state of the atmosphere Bell pro- ceeded to go down. When he got about ten feet down witness sow him" drop all at once." Witness said to Payne" The man is cftowning." Payne replied : "No, he is working," but a moment later exclaimed," Oh, my God, he is." Questioned by the Coroner, witness said the well was 17feet deep and contained five feet of water. Payne said something about a light before Bell went down ti e well, but witness did not understand what he meant. Do you know how long the well has been covered up? —52 years. Neither of these men did anything to ascertain that the atmosphere of the well was pure ?—No. William Woods tanner, Heath-road, Penketh, stated that on hearing ;hat Bell and Payne had fallen down the well, he lighted a candle to test the atmosphere of the well. The candle would not burn 18 inches below the surface. Grappling irona were obtained and the bodies were recovered at 7.40 p.m. Dr. Murray, of Penketh, was present when the bodies were brought to the surface, and tried artificial respiration on both for an hour and a half. Mr. Connor: How long was it after the accident that you came to the well? Witness: I should think a quarter of an hour or twenty minutes. Something had to be done to the pump, and a man in his employ was working at it, as sufficient water could not be obtained. He was at work at the pump two days before the accident happened. The man had made the pump worse rather than better. The Coroner: He is not the only tradesman who has done that. Continuing, witness said he sent George Bell to the faun because hs bad the reputation of being a good man at pumps, and he left him to do what was right in the circumstances.

I don't want to say anything to hurt your feelings, but was it not a foolish thing to strip a well which had been covered all these years and at once go down with- out taking any precaution ?—Yes, sir.

"Will you attempt it, Miss Carisforde ? It is quite safe," Captain Desmond says, leaning towards her. _ _ _ _

"Oh, no—oh, I dare not !" and Nina looks back helplessly at him. "I should fall." "Then we must return."

"Oh, yes, the ladies all worship . him, think him divine, I believe. I can't say I think him particularly interesting myself." A very merry party gather in the Oakwoods even Mrs. Norris has been persuaded to go." Nina Carisforde, looking very fair and shadowy in her white dress claims Captain Desmond's attention. She is heartless and cruel, and has decided that this man is worth winning. She puts forth all her art of fascination, which she is wont to boast has never failed yet on mortal man. He, without a thought of disloyalty to Coral, yields himself up to the fascination of the moment. Meanwhile poor Coral's heart is aching, though she, gives no sign, but laughs and talks merrily with the others. Her cheek is flushed and her eyes bright. A casual observer would detect nothing amiss, but to the eyes of love it is different, and Cyril Clifford, looking on the face of the girl he secretly adores, experiences a very unelerical desire to punish the man who is making her suffer thus. "Captain Desmond is paying very marked attention to Miss Carisforde, " remarks Mrs. ,Vilna to Mrs. Norris, as the two sit together under the trees sipping tea and discussing the scandal of the neighbourhood. "I wonder Coral likes it."

"Oh, Coral does not mind: she is not sensi- • not like our family. Dear Nina, now, would • of grief if she were slighted, but I believe Coral would only laugh, and go Its usual with her horses and dogs; she is so unwoiranly." " she goes on to herself. "What is he thinking of if he prefers that painted butterfly to her?" "Oh, Coral," Ada Willis calls out presently, "here Comes a gipsy. Do let us have our fortunes told; it will be such fun."

"Miss Carisforde, do you believe the sibyl has power to read the book of fate?" Captain Desmond asks.

"No, of course not. It is all nonsense," Nina answers, laughing. "Let me read the future for you, ladies and gentlemen," the woman says. Coral gazes, as if fascinated, on the dark, handsome face and clear, penetrating oyes that look as if they were indeed fixed on the future sho professes to reveal. Nina gives the required piece of silver, and holds out her small white hand for the sibyl's inspection. "You have a fair, smooth life, lady," she says. "There is no trouble for you, but you will cause plenty to others. You have ruined men's souls and broken women's hearts, and you'll break more. Be warned by the gipsy, and give up what you now have in view; give it up, I say, or you'll work more ruin than you can mend."

Coral is the last. "Come away, Miss Caris- forde," Cyril Clifford implores, in a low voice. "Leave the future alone; she cannot reveal it, and will only frighten you." "No, I must not turn coward, Mr. Clifford," Coral says, smiling. The gipsy takes the girl's hand, looks at it a moment, then gazes pityingly, almost tenderly, into the lovely- dark face. "Poor child, poor child—so young too!" she mutters. Aloud she says: "There's trouble in 'store for you, sore trouble; you'll never marry the Iran you've set your heart on. You've a brave heart, but there's sore trouble and an early death for you; you might have made a fortune by your voice, but you will not. You will stake your all on one die and lose it, and it will break your poor heart, child." "Come away, Coral," Captain Desmond say, noticing how white the girl has become. "How ean you listen to such nonsense ? " "My fine gentleman, the sibyl must tell the truth. You yourself will cause her grief," the gipsy says, as she walks off and disappears among the trees. Wandering from the others by a large tree the girl sinks down on the moss, sobbing bitterly. !'l. do not mind anything else, only she said I should lose your love," Coral sobs. LALL BIGHM 111.43 to It Mt%

A SUMMER CLOUD.

BY ELLA BECKLEY,

tuthor of "Duleie Bruce," "The Pretty Maid of Malton," "Kissing Goes by Favour," "How I Saved Cynthia Milton," &c.

It is a bright morning in June; the sky is cloudless, and the sun streaming down on the old _ _ . _

churchyard on the hillside, on the moss-grown graves, and ivy-covered tower of the church. On the grass under one of the spreading trees a girl is sitting, sketch-book in hand—a girl, fair and sweet as the daisies blooming at her feet —the golden sunshine lighting up her brunette beauty as she raises her violet eyes from her task. A man's footstep sounds on the gravelled path. "Oh, Conrad, how you startled me!" she says. "I did not hear you coming. Look at this stupid thing; I can't make it come right." Be removes the leaf containing the half-finished drawing from the book, presses it reverently to his lips, and places it in his pocket-book. Then, with a few deft strokes of the pencil, is busily engaged on a fresh sketch, while the girl's eyes rest with fond pride on his face. Coral is very proud of her handsome lover, with his dreamy blue eyes and tawny moustache. "Scampering over the country on barebacked horses is more in your.line, isn't it, Coral ? " he says. "Yes; and that reminds me, I promised old Bob to try that new colt this morning." "I wish you would leave Bob to attend to the horses, Coral. It is unsafe. I wonder your father allows it," Captain Desmond says, rather impatiently. , 'Papa does not mind, and I thought you did not," Coral says, ,deprecatingly. He softens at once, and takes her hand tenderly in both his own. "It is all right while I am near to be with you _ _ _

and look after you, but I shall be so anxious when I am far away from you." "What do you mean? Arc you going away ? " "We are ordered to Ireland. The news came this morning." "Ireland! Oh, that is so far away. I shall never see you. Oh! Conrad, must you 'go ? " the ; girl says, piteously, her eyes filling with tears at thought of the parting. "Don't cry, my darling," Captain Desmond says, fondly. "Ireland is not the end of the world. I shall be able to run over from there to see you, and I hope to gain your father's consent to an early marriage, and then there will be no question of parting again. You will write often, will you not ? " "Oh, yes, but letters will not be like having you to talk to. Perhaps you.will forget me." I "Forget you! That is not possible, my Coral." They have left the churchyard now, and are walking slowly along the leafy lane leading to Coral's home. "We have a fortnight yet, I believe." The girl gives a sigh of relief. It is a respite. The parting is still in the distance. Youth is elastic, and at the thought she becomes almost happy again, and they saunter on absorbed in each other and love's young dream. "Here is Mr. Clifford coming," Coral says presently, as a tall figure in clerical garb appears in sight round the bend of the road. ; "Confound that fellow! He is always turning up when he's not wanted. I beg your pardon, Coral; but, really, we never seem to be together five minutes without that parson appearing." "Five minutes ! " Coral laughs. "At least it has been two hours this morning. Conrad, I believe you are actually jealous of Mr. Clifford," and again she laughs merrily. "Small Wonder if I am," he retorts. "He is always dancing attendance on you. The fellow is in love with you, Coral, that is quite evident—and I am leaving you here to meet him every day." "Oh! Conrad, you do not really think that I care a straw for him, or anyone but you?" Coral says, earnestly. The Rev. Cyril Clifford is a tall, pale young man, with fair hair and large blue eyes, the idol of all the ladies, old and young, of his congre- gation. Enthusiastic and energetic, the duties of the parish devolve chiefly on him. "I was coming to see you this afternoon," the curate says, as he turns back with them. "I want to see Mr. Carisforde, too, if he is at home. I want to ask him about old Mrs.

Jones." "Hang those old '.women ! " mutters Captain Desmond under his breath, making vigorous onslaught on the nettles by the roadside with his stick. "Can the fellow talk of nothing else ? " "Come this evening," Coral is saying in her sweetest tones. "The Willises are coming for tennis. Ah ! here is Ulric," as a lovely boy Gf seven or eight years old comes bounding down the carriage drive leading to the front door of the Beeches. In contrast to Coral's brunette beauty

===== 0002601_19070629_art0032.txt | included=true | included =====
Desmond's departure. Nina Carisforde has laid her plans well. 'Conrad tells himself he still [lopes to make Coral his wife, but he is genuinely fascinated by this beautiful siren. The Carisfordes haze visitors this evening. and Desmond has escaped into the garden with hi* cigar. The night is hot, and all is very stilt; the sound of laughter and voices reach his ears from the open windows of the drawing-room, then the sound of someone singing. He stops to listen as Coral's magnificent voice is borne to him on the still night air. The song ceases, and he wanders on further from the house. Presently the rustle of a woman's dress falls on his ear, and he turns to see Nina Carisforde, the moonlight falling on her plat-blue dress and golden hair. In an instant he flings away his half-smoked cigar and turns to greet her. "And so you are leaving us to-mierrow, Captain Desmond ?_ _ she says, in her dulcet tones.

_ "Yes. May I flatter myself that you Will miss me a little?" he answers, in a low voice.

"Indeed I shall, very much. Our friendship has been very pleasant; but all pleasant things come to an end."

"1 should not have imagined that to be your experience. But why should our friendship be at an end because lam going to Ireland? I hope to return, and; you know, I shall be a relative by-and-bye." "Oh! why do you remind me of that? Oh, it is cruel—cruel!" And Nina raises a lace- trimmed handkerchief to her eyes and sobs audibly. Captain Desmond looks at her for a moment in puzzled wonder. Then the truth bursts on him— thi.4 beautiful creature loves him. Fate is cruel! Is it his fault, he wernders. Nina sobs On. Clasping her hands round his arm she lets her golden head fall on his shoulder in abandonment of grief. "For Heaven's sake come away!" he says, unsteadily. She allows him to put his arm round her and lead her away. But it is too late. Coral, seeking her cousin at Aunt Helen's request, had arrived in time to see the golden head of her rival pillowed on hor lover's shoulder. That glimpse is enough. One moment the girl stands as if turned to stone, the next she has fled.

Oh! Captain Desmond, what must you think of me ? " Nina says, raising her blue eyes still full of tears to his face.

"I am so sorry, Miss Carisforde. What a brute you make me feel What can I do?" "Nothing, I suppose. You are going to marry Coral ?" "And you love her?"

"As my life," the man says. hoarsely. "You have let me love you," Nina wails, "and now you will leave inc to bear the pain," and again she sobs bitterly. This is more than Captain Desmond can bear, and he passes his arm round the bowed figure and presses his lips to the golden hair and snowy forehead. .

"For Heaven's sake don't cry like this!" he says.

_ "You do not care. You only love Coral, and I am sure she does not care for you. She is always with Mr. Clifford."

At the touch of his lips on her hair .Nina feels that her cause is won. She raises her head.

"Forgive me ! " she says, more calmly. "If we can be no nearer, we may still be friends," holding out her hand, which he takes eagerly. Manlike, he is thankful that the storm of sobs is over.

"May I ask you one question? Will you answer me plainly and truthfully?" she asks. • " Yes ; anything you 1 ike."‘ "If—if thing's were different," she says, hesi- tating and drooping her eyes—"if you were not engaged to Coral, would you have loved me?" "Yes," he says, "1 should. lam not sure that I don't now, but Av.; must both try to forget. Now let me take you in."

Never has Coral looked more beautiful than he does to-night. There is a flush on her cheek and a light in her eye, the effects of the fever burning in the girl's heart. She is wearing a black lace dress, with coral trimmings and sprays' of coral in her dark hair. As she is to-night Conrad Desmond will remember her for all time. Her spirits are wild too—she is the life of the party. Her lover has no chance of speaking to her alone, and when he seeks her for a few last. words before leaving she is nowhere to be found. "Miss Coral asked me to give you this, sir," the parlourmaid says, holding out a small square envelope. Desmond hastily tears it open and reads "1 saw you with Nina," Coral writes. "I know that your love has gone from me to her. I telt you now, as I have often told you before, I cannot, will not live without your love. I know- that, loving her, you would still for honour's sake make me your wife, so I put it out of your power. When you receive this I shall be dead,, and you will be free. Forget me and be happy.— CORAL."

"Oh! sir, there has been a dreadful accident. Miss Coral has fallen into the lake." It is another frightened maidservant who speaks: "They are afraid sho is dead." "Where is she ? " Desmond asks, hoarsely, great beads of perspiration standing out on his forehead.

"I think they have taken her to her room, sir. Mr. Clifford and the gardener got her out," the girl answers. Desmond waits io hear no more. Half dazed he rushes away to the door of Coral's room. "Is she alive? Let me go to her," he says,, lifting his haggard _eyes to the doctor's face.

"My dear slr, it is-useless," Dr. Douglas says,, gravely. Conrad Desmond sinks down on a seat and covers his face with his hands. Coual—bright, merry Coral—dead! And she will never know now that he really loved her. Bitterly he curses his blind folly, and in the anguish of his heart almost curses Nina too.

The house is turned into a place of mourning. Airs. Norris sits in the darkened drawing-room, sipping sal volatile, applying smelling-salts to her nose, and complaining of the shock to her. nerves.

"YOU saw the accident? " Desmond asks,. meeting Cyril Clifford on the staircase. "Yes, I saw it. But it was no accident,. Captain DeFunond, and you know it," the clergyman answers, sternly. "I do not know to what extent you are to blame for this, but I believe Miss Carisforde's death lies at your door!"

Pc,or Coral is laid to rest in the churchyard —in the churchyard where but a few short months before, awaiting her lover, she had sat: and bketched the ivy-mantled church tower. [THE END. ]

The Recent Prosecution of a Painter. MO APPEAL TO THE HIGH COURT.

meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians was held on Wednesday. In the absence of the chair- man and vice-chairman. Mr. A. J. Jolley was elected to the chair and there were also present :—Mrs. Wilkinson, Mrs. Simpson, Miss Broadbent, Rev. M. Ryan, Messrs. W. Gill, W. Furness, W. C. Brooks, A. Brooks, G. 'lllather, J. Cook, W. It lennagan, M. W. Seton, J. Evans, C. Sutton (clerk), J. F. Awbery (master), and A. Wright (superintendent of Padgete schools). . THE HOUSE COMMITTEE AND THE CASE. THE EMPLOYMENT OF HOUSE LABOUR.

At a meeting of the House Committee held on June 14th James Clarke's case was considered and Mr. Shearer reported the magisterial proceedings which had resulted in Clarke's dismissal. After discussion it was resolved; by a majority that the Clerk be instructed to take steps I for securing front the High Court a mandamus direct- the Warrington Bench to convict Clarke who was charged with neglecting to maintain himself, if after inquiry no legal obstacles to such action be discovered. At a meeting of the House Committee on June 21st it was resolved to recommend that the Clerk be instructed to inquire of the Master Builders in town whether the ratement of an inmate (Thos. Hind.) was correct that. 46 was denied employment owing to his having done Plastering work whilst in the Workhouse. A number of letters in reply to the Clerk's request, Were read, and it was stated that to the writers' know- ledge no such refusal was made. Mr. Collin, president Of the Warrington Master Builders' Federation, wrote stating that he had not refused Hind° work at all, nor had Hinde applied, so far as he (Mr. Collin) could learn. He did not think any builder would refuse him work, provided that he had any work suitable for him' to do. He(Mr.Collin) would respectfully point out that at would be very much better whenever the Board had any repairs in the House to do,if they employed builders in the town instead of employing Union labour, as that would help to keep good practical men from going into the House. The CLERK then presented the following statement on the case of James Clarke:—From the Board's officers and upon perusal of the reports in the local press I find the particulars of Clark's prosecution to have been as follows :—Upon information laid by your officer Clarke was charged that being a person able to maintain him- self ha failed to do so and became an inmate of the workhouse and chatgeable to the Union. Ido not find that any objection was raised to the information, nor tO Clarke being in law liable to conviction and in)prisonment. On the contrary the charge against him appears to have been heard and decided on the merits. Had the court acted otherwise it would have been com- Petent for your officer to have asked for a case upon any legal point in dispute, and in the event of refusal by the justices steps could have been taken to enforce them to grant a case. What occurred was this: Having heard in full the 'avidence against him Clarke was called upon to make his defence when he pleaded that "he was destitute When he went into the Workhouse, and he could not get work. He stopped working on account of the wet Weather." .It was for the justices, and for the justices only, to decide upon the reasonableness of the defend- nut's statement. Any criticism upon their judgment, ;f their action is viewed to have been indiscreet, can nave no effect upon their decision. It does not, how- ?ver, follow that the justices accepted Clarke's statement. may explain at this point that for many years in Part preceding the incorporation of the borough and in Part preceding and following the grant of a separate commission of the peace I assisted in advising county and borough justices, and it was their invariable rule to ?onfer with me upon the degree of punishment, upon 'ha Bench being satisfied of the proof of the guilt of an accused person liable to imprisonment for his offence, nd it would occasionally occur that the justices would 'naSider the circumstances to justify a dismissal with- ;.nt conviction and without sending the offender to gaol in the light of that experience and upon considera- tion of the authority legally possessed by magistrates at the present day I refer your Board to another statement of Clarke which may have influenced the decision of the justices. Clarke said "he had not cost the Guardians a penny during the whole time he had been there and he had praetically done (meaning Painted) the workhouse two or three Hum over." In reply to a justice your officer properly admitted "that Probably the defendant made £1 a week more than he Cost the Guardians." Admitting that, in the eye of the lave, his work did mot save kim from liability to imprisonment had the magistrates have so decided, it Izannot be denied that his plea was one fairly within the authority of the justices to consider in arriving at their decision. I do not think that a mandamus would lie in a case heard and disposed of on the merits only; yet if It did, it would be competent for the justices to reply to a rule of the superior courts that their decision was that the offence appeared to them to be one too light for imprisonment of the defendant, and that the law empowered them so to decide. The CHAIRMAN: Do you suggest he ought not to have been convicted? The CLERK: I submit all through the case that the magistrates could have convicted but I sill they were not bound to convict. It is within their discretion. Mr. EVANS : The High Court leaves it there. The CLERK : The High Court cannot interfere. They can only be appealed to if there is a departure from the spirit of the law. Had the magistrates refused to hear the case or decided that the defence over-ruled the law, then we could have gone against them and the ~ nigh Court would have given judgment upon it. But in a case where they raise no technical or legal objec- tion of any kind but hear the case pro and con, they are luite entitled, even if the defendant be guilty, to come to the conclusion not to inflict imprisonment upon him. Mr. MATHAR said he thought after hearing the Clerk's statement, the safest and wisest plan would be to leave the case where it was. He proposed that no further, action be taken. Mr. SIIToN seconded. Mr. EVANS said the point involved was what the Board were to do if they found a case of persistent imposition. Even if a case were dismissed on its merits, if there was fresh evidence he believed the Court was bound to adjudicate uponit. The Cum( said the 'defendant might plead that be had been already tried and dismissed oa that charge, but if they could, without reference to the previous decision, find that there was a continuous and new .offence, that the man could be employed and 'refused to go to the work, it would he a fresh 'ease altogether and he could not make the 'aanse defence. Clarke was not a burden to the rate- 'Payers, because an officer of the Board admitted that he not only earned his living, but made £1 a week beyond that. He (Mr. Sutton) suggested that there was some- thing in the Master Builders' letter, and that the Beard 'should not put these inmates in employment when they .cante into competition with workmen in the town who had to contribute to the rates out of which these men Were relieved, but that they should put them to degrad- ing employments that were used in order to make the -Workhouse a deterrent, and not an inviting place to a Man who became chargeable. The CHAIRMAN said he wanted to say clearly and distinctly that the statement that Clarke's work had secured the Guardians a profit of over £1 a week THIS *imply ridiculous. Mr. Evaes : I did not believe it. , Mr. Come said he understood Clarke left work to go into the Workhouse. The CLARK said that 'Clarke having worked for his Master for a period, wanted to go oft for a few days. The master refused to allow him, whereupon he went away on his own account. It would not be quite correct therefore to say he left the employment to go to the Workhouse. On the contrary he left to have the holiday which the master refused him. It was an ,?ct of gross impropriety on his part but he would not aave in view at the moment he left, that he was going to the Workhouse. The CHAIRMAN : It was not the Workhouse. It 'was the public house. Arr. EvaNs suggested that the matter be referred to the House Committee so that some practical way might be found of dealing with any future difficulty. The proposition that the case be allowed to drop was carried. , The CmurtmAN said that any question of policy n I.ould be brought before the House Committee. The incident was now closed. The replies of the master builders with regard to "-hoinas Hinde's statement were i eferred to the House 'Committee.

WALKING DAY. HOW THE DAY WAS SPENT.

Returns from the Schools

Below we give statistical information with regard to the various schools, together with particulars of how the day was spent :

Church of England.

137.4UE COAT SCHOOL :---Established 1677 ; number of scholars on books 40 boys, 30 girls; average attend- ance, 70; number of teachers, 3 ; superintendent and matron, Mr. and Mrs. Rose. The children took part in the procession headed by the school band and after- wards attended service at the Parish Church. They were visited by guardians and friends in the afternoon. Special tea was provided and games were enjoyed in the school grounds in the evening. PADGAGE COTTAGE HOMES :—Established 1881. Number of scholars on books, 100 boys 110. girls ; average attendance, 210 ; number of teachers. 4 and superimendent ; chaplain, Rev. W. Pollock-Hill; superintendent, Mr. Arthur Wright; matron, Mrs. Wright; and 12 officials. Afterwards the Church of England scholars attended service at the Parish Church. In the afternooa there were games, &c. on a field lent by Mr W. Houghton and an excellent tea was served. FAIRFIELD (Gums) :—Number of scholars on books, 460; number of teachers, 30 • clergy, the Rector; superintendents, Miss E. White and Miss Willis. The " open Bible" was carried on a blue cushion. After procession, the chil- dren went to Moore by the noon train. Sports and games were indulged in, a field having having been kindly lent for the purpose by Mr.Miffington of Moore. Tea for elder scholars,parents and friends was provided at the farm. The scholars' refreshments were served on the tield.Prizes were ekistributed,and anniversary songs joined in at the close of the day, the party proceeding home at 8.50 p.m.An interesting event was the presenta- tion to Mr. W. Dickens, retiring superintendent of the Sunday school. Mr. Dickens has been associated with simmanuel Church and Sunday school since its com- ment, and has filled almost every position of honour the Church can offer with conspicuous ability and untiring zeal. For many years Mr. Dickens, was the leader or the Young Men's Bible Class, where be endeared him- self to all the members and friends He also filled the position of churchwarden, representing the congrega- tion for some years, and for the two years last past has exercised an affectionate and Christian oversight and control over the Sunday school. His genial and kindly manner at all times won for him many friends, whilst his sturdy and pronounced Protestant churclunanship, and loyalty to be principles of the Reformed Episcopal Church ensure for him the greatest respect of all who have been associated with him. Quite recently Mr. Dickens had to leave the town, removing to IN orthwich. The congregation felt desirous of recognizing his work, sue his visit to the school on Walking Day afforded a suitable opportunity for so doiug.Surrounded by his old scholars end many friends, Rev. F. Vauban on behalf of the Congregation presented Mr.. Dickens with a fountain pen and a purse of gold, assuring him of their regret at parting with him, and of the place he holds in the minds of the coegresat ion he has served so well. Other friends also spoke and Mr. Dickens suitably responded.

ALL THE TORTURES!

A GARDENER'S STORY OF INDIGESTION, SUFFOCATION, MISTS BEFORE THE EYES, PAIN, WIND, SICKNESS. .

MISERY TURNED TO JOY BY RIOTHER SEIGEL'S SYRUP.

That the purest of air and the healthiest of occupa- tions are no absolute guarantee against indigestion, is well shown in the following story. Mr. W. G. P Bruce, of 18, Alma Terrace, -Bawdsey-on-Sea, Wood- bridge, is a gardener; and Bawdsey, where he lives and works, a delightful Suffolk village nestling in flowers and swept by the strong salt winds of the North Sea. Yet Mr. Bruce had suffered from very childhood, and not till he took Mother Seigel's Syrup did he find any relief. Under date January let, 1907, he says : "As a boy I was delicate, and since then I have endured all the usual tortures of indigestion, with many more, of which few I hope have any experience. I bav, had to get out of bed in the piddle qf the night with a dreadful feeling nt my heart,and my breath almost gone. Again, while talking with some one a feeling would come over me that I was going mad, andthat I must get away anywhere but away. At times,while in churob,mist, would form before my eyes till I could not see the people in front of .me. I could not have found my way out, and dared not move till the awful sensation passed. "Pain at my stomach and in my back, wind, head. ache, and vomiting of a nasty sour fluid, were quite common with me. But ,I am glad to say that I have not had an attack for four years now. Mother Seigel's Syrup not only cured me, but keeps me well."

Mother Seizes Syrup will cure you also, If you will but give it a trial. In all parts of the world people know its worth; it you do not know try it; health must follow.

THE EDUCATION QUESTION.

Roman Catholics and the Government Attitude.

MR. CROSFIELD M.P., AND THE INSPECTION OF CONVENTS.

There-was a large attendance at a pablic meeting held in the Cu-operative Hall, Ssnkey-street. on Sunday evening under the auspices of the IVa.rrington branch of the Catholic Young Men's Society. Councillor Owen Toole presided and was supported by' Mr. W. Niadden, Dr. E. Fox, Councillor T. Burke, of Liverpool (vice-president of the Catholic Young Men's Societies of great Britain), Rev. Father Buggies. 0.5.13. (chaplain) Messrs. J. Boyle, C. Roddy, W. Kilduff, R. Archer, T. Newnes, P. Delaney, J. Black, M. Cunningham, M. Flynn, T. KapHun, H. McNiullen, and H. Rigby.

THE CdAIRMAN AND MR. McKENNA'S BILL. The CHAIRMAN said that Catholics who were taking interest in passing events must feel alarmed at the trend of things during the past twelve or eighteen months. Certainly not duriug his lifetime had the enemies of the Mother Church been so militant as they were at the present time. rhey (the Catholics) thought last year they had for ever killed and decently buried that monster of an Education Bill that threatened to Stake from them sacred trusts and rights, which they denied to any Government. (Applause). Their minds were not long left at ease. Another form of an Education Bill was sought to be thrust epos them known as "The McKenna Bill." A more grossly unjust and dishonest bill, to his mind, was never proposed—a Bill which in substance would penalize them for teaching in their schools that which their schools primarily were built for, namely Catholic ' religion. Mr. McKenna, by some sort of arithmetic, ' had worked out that Catholic teachers were during the time they gave to teaching religious matter, wasting 1-15th of the ratepayers' money. As he (Councillor Toole) mentioned at a previous meeting on the educa- tion question, Catholics paid as much in rates and taxes directly and indirectly, as they received back from the Government for their schools' assistance, mid the imposing of this penalty upon them would mean that they would be debarred, except at their own expense,to teach their religion in their own schools, while at the same time they would have, through their rates, to subsidize forms of religion in other schools which they did not approve of. (Applause). Then the Education Department paid no rent for Catholic schools, nor for the repairs to them. Be (Councillor Toole) maintained, even if the argument had any truth in it, that having their schools rent free and repairs done, more than compensated for the pro- portion of the time given by the Catholic teachers to religious matters. On the top of all these things, the Prime Minister the other day said that it was the Government's intention next year to introduce an Education Bill which they were determined to carry. All he (Councillor Toole) could say was that Catholics had no intention of being offensive to. any Govern- ment, or to any denomination, but they respected the rights and consciences of every denomination, and in turn they demanded that the State should protect their consciences. (Applause.) On education matters Catholics of all shades of politics were united,inasmuchl as the faith of their children was at stake. There could be no such word as" Compromise." There was acertain section of the community who, if it, were left to them, would not allow the Word of God to be mentioned in any school, and no matter what political or other party those men belonged to, Catholics must resist to their utmost this spirit—a spirit that had left Prance in the chaos that she was in to-day. Only the other day one of the reasons given why there should be no religious teaching in schools was that the churches were I on. the downward grade' that not so many people attended church as used to do, and that the State had no right to assist religion. Of course they knew that Catholics got no financial assistance from the State, rather the other way, and if they got back what used to belong to them they could afford tovun their own schools But as to the churches being on the down grade, he found that it was very ranch the other way with Catholics. In an official history of the diocese of Liver- pool he saw that they had is Warrington, in the year 1819, one church, one priest (Dr. Molyneux), &tad 900 Catholics. In 1907 they had five churches .12 priests, I an 13,009 Catholi6s. (Applause.) The population of Warrington during the same period hal increased four and a half times—from 15,000 to 70,000. The Catholic population had increased nearly 15 times. So when he I told his friend of that he would need to qualify his remark. Nevertheless, that spirit existed, and no doubt it was due to that, that a Catholic Labour candidate was opposing another Labour candidate in Jarrow. No better friends had the Labour Party had in Parliament than the Irish Party, and the Irish Party in turn had had no better friends than the Labour Party. But there was one question upon which they did not agree—a question upon which the Irish Party, essentially a Catholic Party, felt more strongly upon than any political question. and that was the question of their religion. Immediately the Labour Party took up the qvestion of driving religion out of the,schools, the Irish Party had to sever themselves from them. The war between them had started, and politically they knew which would survive. It was necessary for Catholics to preserve their independence,' and when they had to assert a principle or to defend a right, it WAS necessary for them to be in constant touch with each other, acting together under one organization, ,and that organization to his mind, was one such as the Young Men's Catholic Society of Great Britain (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19070629_art0049.txt | included=true | included =====
A RESOLUTION.

Dr.. Pox, having made the announcement,which was received with loud applause, of the total proceeds of the Infirmary bazaar, expressed his curiosity as to how certain gentlemen on the platform who took a promi- nent part in the last election would explain their posi- tion in view of recent events. They knew now how the Liberal party stood. It would become the duty of Catholics to decide what they intended to do in the future. (Applause). He hoped that meeting would inaugurate the rise of a Catholic party, whic:, wodd not be either a Conservative or Liberal party, bat which would be ready to throw in its lot on either side, according as Catholic interests predominated. were told a general election was forecasted within a short time, and no doubt they would have a meeting to discuss the position which the Catholic party intended to take up. The Catholics of Warrington could decide what Member they would send to Parliament. (Applause). He moved the following resolution :—We Catholics here assembled view with grave apprehension the recent utterances of the Prime Minister and the Minister of Education in reference to education, and we resolve, that we will not tolerate any future legislation which will deprive us of the right to have our children educated in accordance with our religious convictions, and we hereby pludge ourselves to resist to the utmost, any legislation or action of any local authority, which takes away from us the right to have for our children, Catholic religious instruction during school hours, given by Catholic teachers, in Catholic schools and under Catholic management, or that would impose any but Catholic teachers on the regular staff of our schools, or which would impose any disabilities on Catholic teachers,or which would cripple the development of any extension of our Schools, Training Colleges and Sentinaries.

Mi. W.KILDITP's seconded. People said that Ciltholics did not understankthe Bible but they knew one 'passage of Scripture which',said : "Render unto Cesar the thlngs that are Cwsar's and unto God the things that are Gcd's," and they would be prepared to stand by that. Their claim was" Catholic schools for Catholic children." (Applause.) The resolution was carried unanimously. MR. MADDEN AND THE GOVERNMENT.

Mr.Maprart in the course of a long speech said Dr. Fox had thrown out a challenge and hiLwas not in the slightest degree afraid to accept it. Dr. 'Fox said he came there with some curiosity as to what would be said by those who took up a certain attitude during the last election. He (Mr. Madden) appealed to the records in the news- papers to show that the attitude taken up by the Irish Catholics of the town was plain and clear. Never once from first to last during the whole of that election did the Liberal party dare to introduce on the platform a single word on the Education question. (Applause.) He knew further that they were true and loyal to their compact with himself when he told them that if there was a man from the candidate or the President of the Liberal Association downwards who would dare to say one word against the principles that they were advocat- ing for Catholic education, Ile (Mr. Madden) would publicly contradict him on Mr. Crosfield's platform. Ho wondered how many men were ready to do the same thing to-morrow—to stand up on a platform in the midst of political friends or enemies and stand to their guns, no matter what others might say. They need have no fear as to what his (Mr. Madien's) Catholic attitude would be. He knew perfectly well that he as far as be could, extracted, along with other members of the Catholic body in Warrington, a promise from the Member as to what he would do on the question of Catholic Education. From want of Catholic organiza- tion in England, when the election was sprung upon the people, only at the last minute was the question settled by the Bishops leaving them to put questions to the candidates and please themselves how they voted, according as the answers of the candidates satisfied their consciences. If there had been that organization among the Catholics which there should have been, if they had been up and doing and if there had been more loyalty to one another and more sympathy and meeting together between the ecclesiastics and the prominent laymen of the Church, they would have had a•front that nothing could have stood against.(Applause.)He claimed his right as a Catholic layman to have his voice in the shaping of Catholic policy in this country and he was not going to be a party to any, election in which he was ordered to do this or that. Ho was going to exercise his rights as a.citizen. He had satAcient confidence in, himself to know that he would never do anything in the slightest degree to besmirch the beauty and the glory of the banner of their faith. (Applause.) In dis- eussiug the Catholic attitude they could not get rid of the fact that N per cent. or more of the Catholics were Irishmen and Irishwomen. Although

they were all Catholics, the 10 per cent. could not turn round on the 00 and say "You must subordinate all your views and political aspirations, all your ideas of civil government and your share in the government of the country, and give way to us and not have the courage to express your views." The Liberal party was the one to which the Irish, had been allied for many years, and from whom they had received great and signal service, and until a little bit of recent legislation in the shape of the Land Act he would ask whether, outside the religious question, a single meritorious Act had ever been done by the Con- servative party for the Irish people. The Conservative record was one of tyrinny and oppression in Ireland. They were the ones to drive out the people of Ireland till they came to this country, and that was wby they had 12,000 Catholics in ‘Varrington now. Catholics were as determined tc-day as ever they were, and on. the question of education they were the only ones who had shown any backbone. (Applause.) There was no compromise in the Irish character, and that was why the other day they rejected the miserable limitation of a Home Rule Bill. It was because there was no cempromise in their character that the claims of Rome and of the Catholic Church were safe in the hands which were now defending them. (Applause) Mr. Madden pleaded for a federation of Catholics. pointing out that at present they were a broken and and disintegrated mass. There should be one parent booty front which all the branches should draw their lifiblaod and they should work together. A poor, deluded creature had been found to bring into Parliament a Bill for the inspection of convents. Tbeir own common sense might tell them that if there was the slightest desire on the part of any of these women who had devoted themselves to the service of God, to escape from a confinement which they looked upon as imprisonment, there would have been no difficulty whit. ever in leaving the convents. The member for Warrington was asked whether be voted for or against the Bill, and it appeared that Mr. Crosfield did not vote as he Was not in the House at the time.

MR. CROSFIELD'S LITTER. In the coarse of a letter Mr. Crosfield said :—"On the day of the debate on the introduction of the Monastic and Conventual Institutions Bill I received a telegram from Warrington asking me how I had voted. I replied with a wire that I had not voted at all. . . . Iliad I been in the Hous• I should certainly have voted against the introduction of this Bill. May I just add a word or, two filltthar on this matter. Suppose some question arose about the inspection of say laundries in - Convents where work was done under conditions that clearly came within the regulations of Government, I think for the • protection of workers inspection might conceivably be quite justifiable in' such cases and I do not suppose for a moment our Catholic friends would take exception to that. As a supporter of trade unions I think I should feel it nry duty to support reasonable inspection of that kind if such a proposition were put before one. This Bill in the House the other 'day I regard as inter- ference with the private religious life of Catholics and I am therefore extremely sorry that the above men- tioned circumstances prevented me voting against it. I shall oppose it and feel sure it will be killed at its second reading." (Applause.) He (Mr. Madden) did not know what Mr. Crosfield was going to do. He would tell them candidly that Mr. Crosfield was no more to him than any other member of Parliament. Mr. Crosfield had kept his pikruise to the Catholics of this town, whatever the opinion might be about him as to whether he was a good member of Parliament or not. In the one essential thing as far as Catholics were concerned Mr. Crosfield bad kept his promise faithfully and loyally. Before Dr. Fox wanted to know what he (Mr. Madden and other gentlemen on the plat- form were going to do in future, he would ask him to go to the Catholic Bishops of England and to the Catholic Educatioa douncil, and ask them to organize the Catholic body, to agree to a programme and place it before them, and if they wanted the people to accept it, let some of them have seats on the Education Council so that that organisation might have the benefit of their experience and knowledge. (Applause). Mr. Madden dealt with the attitude of the Irish party on various questions, and said that no matter what dangers beset the Church and the education of the children, the sacred trust could not be in boiler or more faithful hands than it had been in the past. (Applause).

===== 0002601_19070629_art0050.txt | included=true | included =====
AN APPEAL TO YOUNG MEN.

Councillor BURK' delivered an address on the work of the Catholic Young Men's Society. His plea was on behalf-of the young men of our great towns belonging to the Catholic body who had to live in an atmosphere which was not patholic. They had to face the fact that Catholics were a small minority of the ponulation of this country, and they ran a serious risk of losing the young men unless they were in a definite Catholic organization within the Church. The moment such an Education Bill RS had been mentioned received the assent of the King the Anglican Church would go to smash. In the great series of organizations known as Dissent there was nothing like a definite or clear conception of what Christianity was. Its latest freak was only what might have been expected in the person of their friend Mr. Campbell. Councillor Burke spoke of the impracticability of forming a Catholic party. He could not conceive a political organization composed of Catholics from the Duke of Norfolk down to the man who swept the streets. In these days when so many Catholics were trade unionists and allowed their repre- sentatives to go to the Congress and vote in favour of the secular solution the position were still more corn- ' plicated. What was wanted was an organization in which Catholics could meet together on the common ground of faith. Thera he would Imve no fear of Om ultimate result should they be called upon to vote at the ballot box. Party politics wore excluded from the Young Men's Society. The Catholic young men of Lancashire did not realize the responsibilities of citizenship, but they would if they were inside their society. (Applause). All the political parties had lost their moorings. The great party in this country called the Labour party, with which he was in thorough sympathy, belonged to the group known as the Socialists. The workingtnen in this country who belonged to the Catholic faith were nearly all in the trade unions and the trade unions were being dominated by the Socialists who were the fighting section in the party. Socialists averred that the only, solution of the Edueation question was the secular one and be had known Catholics stand up at the Congress and openly advocate that as the proper solution. There was a necessity for Catholics remaining in trade unions to permeate them with their Catholic prrinciples. (Applause). On the proposition of Mr. DELANEy, seconded by 31r. Rrcpv, and supported by Mr. G. Room', the following resolution was passed :—" This meeting recognizes the past work of the C.Y.M. Society, and considers that this organization has proved itself well qualified to undertake the various works spoken of Mr. Councillor Burke the vice-chairman of the C.Y.M.Society of Great Britain, and the meeting pledges itself to !ippon the society in their various undertakings, especially their registration section." Mr. Rigby mentioned that letters had been received from the Bishops of the diocese of Shrewsbury and Liverpool expressing their entire approval of the work of the society.

The speakers and chairman were thanked for their services on the proposition of the Rev. Father Bugging seconded by Mr. Boyle.

Got What He Wanted.

At Wartington, on Wednesday, Daniel Marsh, who stated he came from Liverpool, was charged with attempting to obtain by false pretences a quantity of picture postcards and cigarettes from the shop of Henry\ Richard Horobin, newsagent, Sankey-street. On Tuesday afternoon prisoner went into the prose- cutor's shop and asked to be slypwn some postcards. He selected a number of cards, and afterwards asked for an ounce of cigarettes, which were supplied. When asked for payment he said he had paid for what he had purchased. Afterwards he refused to leave the shop and created a disturbance. In answer to the charge at the Police Station he said he created a bother to be locked up. The Chairman (the Mayor, Alderman Smethurst) said his request would be granted, and he would have to go to gaol for three months.

The plan for dealing with THE HOUSE the House of Lords, which OF LORDS. the Government since the

collapse of the Education bill last December has hal in preparation, is now Put before the country. The hon. member for 'Warrington, who on Wednesday distinguished himself in the happiest of maiden speeches, stated the fundamental issue before the House succinctly enough—" the pith and essence of the govern- loot's proposal amounted to this—that after due Precautions against hasty legislation, but with- out undue or dangerous delay, the will of the people must be allowed to prevail.'' The chronic hostility of the Lords to plebian tequirements is after all at the very root of this grave question, and the PRIME VENT , in hie speech to the House of Commons on Monday, did not conceal the point of view from which he -his colleagues regard the Upper Chamber. heir main complaint against the House of Lords lie its inherent hostility to Liberal or Radical legislation. That hostility has, they claim. 1/`l,ell demonstrated to the full during the trisklort life of the present Parliament. Sir 4-IENTItv CA.MPBELL :13A.NNERMAN was at SOMA Pains to prove his contention by a series of corn Darisons as to the action of the Upper Ho ise during those', periods in which a Conservative Government has been in power and those when the party which he now leads has held the reins of office. He instanced the comparatively Phott time allowed to the Peers by the late Governm)nt, and azquiesced in by their Lordships,for the considers- tloo. 0f such important measures as the E.-location Act of 1902 and the Licensing Act which followed Prom these examples he deduced, noti Unfairly, the argument that the functions of the Upper House, ad interpreted under a onservative Government,'were not precisely the as those which were exercised during R -Liberal rogime. The 'argument does not need elaboration. It represents what is undoubtedly the strong feeling of Liberals throughout the country, and its justice has not been denied by many Peers of Unionist persuasion, who have "ggested methods by which the permanent character of the party majority in the Hinge of 1-I,°rds might be mitigated or removed. Mr 15ALPourt, in his reply to the Premier, denied that the argument against the House of Lords was Elt/engthed by the action of the Uppor Chamber On the 'Education Bill last year. Indeed, he declared, the fate of the Bill had not aroused in the country the indignation which the Govern - Merit desired to represent.

The immediate matter of concern, however, is llot the actual constitution of the House of Lords Iteelf. In that the Government is not at present 'leePly interested. Its object, as declared in the tesolution moved by the Premier, was to secure that the will of the people, as expressed by the lionse of Oommons, should prevail. What is the method by which it proposes to accomplish this end ? A bill is to be introduced in the House of Commons, and it is to go in the usual way to the trlpPer Chamber. If it is there rejected, a small continence of Members of both Houses is to be tailed, which will endeavour to arrive at Some agreement in which the two Houses may be able to acquiesce. If this conference, eowever, fails, the Government would be enabled after a period of not less than six months to eintroduce the bill in the Commons, pass it through that House by a drastic closure, and send again to the House of Lords. If it is still 113131305 ed in that Chamber a second conference may ,ue called ; and if again there is failure to find a °leeis of agreement the whole process may be tePeated a third time, with the difference that on this occasion the House of Commons will have Polver to pass the bill into law over the heads of the Peers. The plan is unquestionably com• heated, and to the more valiant spirits on the Ministerial side may appear to be an Illlnecessarily long-drawn-out method of dealing :91th:the Upper Chamber. The succession of con- Lerences gives to the scheme an appearance of e°llcession to the Peers which does not suit the ttetePer of many of Sir HENRY CAMPBELL- -1141/NERMAN'S followers. They, like the Labour „,artY, would have preferred the direct method of 'uolition. As Mr. CROSFIELD reminded the House ,(34 Wednesday so moderate a man as Mr. MORLEY Wrote many years ago that he had no very great °°..? of Second Chambers. thtgut if their wishes have not been gratified in t direction, the Government has propounded a ala Which if it were carried into its full effeet
