would, unquestionably, produce the result they desire. A strong Government elected on a wave of popular feeling would be able under this scheme to enforce its will `upon the House of Lords with- out question. Looked at in that light, the veto of the House of Lords under this resolution must disappear entirely, provided the Government of the day is strong enough to control its supporters, and to keep them in line in the face of such compromises as may be suggested during the passage of any bill through Parliament. The real point which the/country has, therefore, to consider is that in effect the proposal of the Government, given these conditions, for the erection of a single Chamber legislature. The House of Lords will become an advisory body, whose advice may or may not be taken, according as the Government desires. Whatever the merits or demerits of the Housell of Lords as a legislative body may be—and few people deny the need for reform—the country will have to consider very carefully a proposal which involves so great and drastic a constitutional change as this. Apparently, the scheme of the Government is to be made a test question at the next General Election. There is plenty of time, therefore, for a full and careful study of the effect which it will produce, not merely upon the next Parliament, but upon the Parliaments. of generations to come. That all Liberals will not agree to it is evident from the criticism to which several Members on the Ministerial side have already subjected it.

In cricket parlance, and to THE MEMBER'S quote Mr. Crosfiold himself, MAIDEN EFFORT. he has at last "made a stroke off his own bat.' We congratulate him most heartily on behalf of W-arrington Liberals on the maiden speech which , he delivered in the House on Wednesday evening. It was a good speech and 'a characteristic speech —temperate in tone and appealing to the reason- ing faculties of the hon. members, who listened attentively, and applauded heartily the various points made by the debutant. It would probably surprise Mr.Crosfield if he could witness the great pleasure felt among his constituents at the fact that he haE. at last "caught the Speaker's eye." They have admitted his contention in the past that there was far too much talking in the House, but with truly human contrariety they have hoped that he would lot his voice be heard in the historic chamber. We should not

like to say that Mr. Crosfield's speech on the House of Lords came as a bombshell to War- rington, because bombshells do not usually con- vey a pleasant "surpriso,—which the news of Thursday morning undoubtedly did. Everybody, except perhaps the bitterest and most disapplinted of Conservatives, is delighted and hopes the Mem- ber will say some more. Evan the man in the street —discontented individual as he generally is—has a pat on the back ready for Mr. Crosfield, who has atoned somewhat for the scarcity of his visits to the "good old town." We should all like to see him, more than ever now that he has com- menced to actively and vocally represent Warring- ton in the counsel a of the nation, and if he is not too closely engaged settling the fate of the House of Lords, we hope he will pay a visit to his con- stituents very soon.

The Guardians do not THE GUARDIANS appear to have relished the AND rife recent decision of the CLARKE CASE. magistrates. in dismissing

James Clarke, charged witli neglecting to maintain himself. It will be remem- bered that Clarke had been employed while in the Workhouse to do odd jobs at painting, and in the Police Court it was admitted by an officer of the Union that "defendant probably made £1 a week more than he cost the Guardians." This statement had obviously a lot to do with the dis- missal of the case. The Guardians were not satisfied and instructed the Clerk to make inquiries with a view to securing a mandamus directing the Warrington Bench to convict Clarke. At the meeting of the Board this week, the Clerk presented a statement on the case in which he up- held the legality of the decision of the magistrates, and pointed out that Clarke was not a burden to the ratenayers because it was admitted that he earned more than his living while in the Workhouse. Mr. Sutton also presumed on his privileged position as an ernert in Poor Law administration to advise the Guardians not to put such men as Clarke in employment by which they came into competition with the workmen in the town who had to contribute to the rates nut of which these men were relieved. The Clerk's advice is in the nature of a very proper admonition for the Guardians appear to have misunderstood their functions as" employers " of the inmates who come to them for support and relief. There is a certain clam of employment for Workhouse inmates, which should not be varied from without very good reason. In this case it was distinctly ungenerous of the Guardians to avail themselves of Clarke's skill as a painter and then prosecute him for neglecting to maintain himself. No wonder, as a result, they find themselves very uncomfortably situated.

There is a touch of sadness HEALTH OF THE about the annual report on BOROUGH. the health of the Borough

which has just been issued, for in its opening sentences reference is made to the lamented death of Dr. Gornall, whose reports were always such models of lucidity and contained a mass of highly valuable information. In the circumstances the duty of preparing the report for 1906 has devolved upon Dr. Prudence E. Gaffikin, the acting medical officer,with the help of the staff, and she explains: " It is unavoidably less full, and more impersonal than in previous years; but, as we hope, a record of the faithful work done by the good man whose loss we do much deplore." Several features of great interest and importance to the well-being of the community are touched upon. The increasing prevalence of the use of artificial preservatives in articles of food is commented upon and Dr. Gaffikin observes: " Unfortunately, as yet, it has not been possible to induce a very serious view of the subject to be taken by those best able to put a stop to the practice in War- rington. Only continued persistence in following up this matter of the use of chemicals as food Preservatives is likely to influenco public opinion." It has unfortunately to be recorded that the infant death-rate of Warrington is distressfully high, viz: 157 per 1,000 births, the greatest number of infant deaths occurring in St. John's Ward. This it is pointed out, is an increase of 10 per 1,000 on that of last year, but the summer of 1906 was exceptionally hot, and the weather is one of the chief determining causes of a high infantile death. rate, especially in relation to epidemic diarrhcel, which, Dr. Gaffikin says it is very much to be wished was a notifiable disease During the last few years efforts have been made by this department to spread some knowledge of the right way to feed young children, but much more could be done if some more regular and definite way of obtaining information as to the occurrence of births was in vogue. A considerable portion of the report is devoted to a consideration of the efforts of the department to deal with consumption. The main difficulty is concerned with notification which, it is contended,should be made compulsory.

Balloons are just now in the air. From RaneTagil

BALIONS.

Gardens during a recent competition almost a dozen of them ascended. Some reputations are like balloons, for the higher in the social scale such persons get the smaller they become to ordinary understandings. In 1871, during the Siege of Paris, no fewer than fifty-four balloon posts were despatched, carrying 2,500,000 letters, weighing ten tons. To the man in the street ballooning represents a mere pastime. It is only in a beleaguered city that the useful work of the aeronaut, with his bag of silk filled with gas, is properly appreciated. The Examiner, Saturday, June 29, 1907

cally we are all balloonists, and send up our pilot" ballons d'essai " to see which way the wind blows when we have a favour to ask of a friend. Whether intentionally or not, nearly all successful people have the gift of trimming their sails so as to meet the opinions of opponents at least half -way. Some people, like balloons, give themselves mighty airs and assume a sureriority which disappears before a stiff breeze. Thus, our prevailing habits or manners, if refined, give us an air of "gentility," and when we air our opinions with a reputation for correctness they are listened to with attention. On the other hand, the con- ceited person, who blows off chaff or, ventilates his grievances without discrimination, is is regarded as a gas-bag; and a Gascon, on the other side of the water, is an absurd boasting braggart. It is said that when such a gas-hag was asked what he thought of the Louvre in Paris he replied : "It is not bad ; it reminds me of the back part of my father's stables." This species of human balloon resembles the firework bearing the same name, which is a ball of pasteboard filled with combustibles. When lighted, it ascends to a con- siderable height, and then explodes in a number of stars, which drop smnts on the.beholders beinw.

The " Beautiful Liverplol " movement is booming and there has been an interesting competition for prizes offered for the most beautifully decorated house fronts. Canon Morley Stevenson is one of the pioneers of the movement in the city. At a conference held on Thursday he rightly raised the question as to why eotporations had not the power to say, "This house is so atrociously ugly that we will not permit it to be put up ?" (Hear, hear). If the local authorities had such a power, they would be spared the spectacle of miserable rows of mean-looking buildings in their large towns. *

The present position of -matters between the Cor- poration and Traders and the Ship Canal Company stands as follows, judging by the report of the Chair- man of the Rivers Committee : The original judg- ment of Mr. Justice Byrne whereby. the Company are compelled to dredge a channel in the river and maintain at all times 8 feet of water, and to carry out all other obligations of the Company under the Act of 1885, and the agreement made between the Corporation and the Company of the oth of April, 1806, has been restored. Furthermore, the petitioners against the Ship Canal Various Powers Bill are protected against an increase of salinity in the water flowing over Woolston Weir. Warrington's rights have been well maintained, though at great cost, and the benefit to the town should be appreciated by posterity. * *,* A very curious fact has been brought to light by the report of the Public Accounts Committee, just issued. It appears that twelve months after the rudder for the battleship Kin, Edward VII. was fitted into the vessel the Admiralty were informed by a dismissed employee of the Ayrshire Foundry Com- pany that a large fault existed in the casting, which toe management of the company had succeeded in concealing. Examination proved that such a defect existed, and the company were called upon to replace the casting. This they did, but a second and a third casting were equally full of flaws and defects. The Admiralty then decided that the cast should be made in the dockyard at the cost of the contractors. The committee state that the pecuniary loss in this case was small, but the conduct of the firm would appear te have offered matter proper for s prosecu- tion for fraud, and they did not understand why some effort was not made to bring the persons to trial. They add, however, that the loss of Admiralty orders and the rumours of this transaction brought such financial loss to the Ayrshire Foundry Company that it shortly became bankrupt. The committee hope that it will not be their duty again to investigate a case where a British firtn for any commercial advan- tage will callously hazard the lives of hundreds of their fellow-countrymen

Cash or easy payments.-1 S. GARLAND, 51 to 55, Bridge-street, Warrington.—Advt. Loyal Order of Ancient Shepherds (AU.) _ _ _ _ _

The Warrington District of the above Order- held its first annual bowling main on Saturday. Three buses were engaged to convey the members' to the "Cat and Lion," Stretton, leaving town about two ;o'clock. On arrival a start was promptly made and the green presented a busy appearance until dusk with the exception of a break about four o'clock when a very heavy storm swept over the green, causing a temporary stoppage of hostilities. Considering the condition of the green after the storm and the cold wind, which made things rather uncomfortable, the bowling was on the whole extremely good, some interesting and exciting gatnes being witnessed. ' The right to hold the "Sir Gilbert Greenall " Challenge Cup for the next twelve months and to possess the gold medal presented by Councillor Fulton-Smith, was earned by Bro. John Appleton, "Old Colt," of the Late Spring Rose Lodge No. 828, who bowled well all through the main, his last win (11-21 being a grand finish to a brilliant performance. His oppo- nent. Bro. J. Adams, of the Olive Branch Lodge No. 1120, fully earned the honour to contest the final, but whether the cold wind had affected him or he was a bit excited by the importance of the contest, his col- lapse in the final after his previous excellent display was a big surprise. The result seemed, however, to be very popular amongst the members, the winner being warmly congratulated all round. Daring the afternoon full justice was done by the members to a substantial tea provided by the landlord (Mr. Robert Walker) which gave every satisfaction. Despite the rather inclement weather the venture was voted a huge success, and the efforts of the committee were warmly appreciated. It is intended to have a smoking con- cert in the Assembly Room, Conservative Club, Sankey-street, when the Cup, medal,and other valuable prizes will he distributed to the winners, to which all the members of the Order will be invited. "LEA-FRANCIS." " PREMIER," and " BUDGE- WHITWORTH " CYCLES. Price from £4 10s. Cash or easy payments.—J. S. GARLA.NO. 51 to 55, Bridge-street, Warrington.—Advt. Meeting of Druids 4 _

The members of the Druids' Camp lodge held their quarterly meeting on Saturday at the Higher Angel, Buttermarket-street, and there were present : P.D.G.M, John Bibby, P.D.G.M. James Wilcock, P.D.G.M. John Woods, P.D.G.M. John Heath; Lodge Secretary, P.D.G.M. Sidney Packer, Lodge Treasurer P.D.G.M. Simnel Hankey, PA. William Woois and a large number of lodge members. At the conclusion of the ordinary lodge business Bro. John Bibby took the chair and called .upon Bro. S. Hankey to present N.G.A. Bro. Hodnett with a framed emblem of the Order in recognition of his having filled the various offices of the lodge satisfactorily, and for work done in connection with the lodge generally.—Bro. Hankey spoke in high terms of Bro. Hodnett's work since he was transferred from Earlestown to them.—' Bros. Packer, William Hankey and John Woods also spoke of Bro. Hodnett's work. Bro. John Bibby then: called upon P.D.G.M. Bro. Samuel Hankey to receive from members of the lodge a beautiful gold-mounted umbrella in recognition of his long and valuable ' services to the lodge. The Chairman briefly dealt with the offices Bro. Hankey held, showing that the members of his lodge must have bad every confidence in him, for besides having hardly been out of office during his 49 years' members:lmo of the lodge, he held at present the offices of District Trustee, Secretary of the Warrington Branch of the Royal Arch Chapter, President of the Board of the Royal Arch Chapter in General, and was a member of the committee of every kindred society in connection with the Order in War- rington. (Applause.) —Bro. S. Packer, the lodge secretary, spoke of the valuable services rendered to the lodge by their treasurer, Bro. Hankey.—Bros. John Woods, Hughes and Derbyshire also spoke in glowing terms of the services rendered by Bro. Hankey. —Bro. Hankey, who was loudly applauded, said that what he had done for his lodge or the district he had Idone cheerfully, and he hoped to be spared to serve them many more years. (Applause.)

NOTES AND NOTIONS.

Warrington Walking Day was specially favoured. It was the sunniest and most summer-like day there has been for over a month. The clerk of the weather may go out on bail! * * There are good berths awaiting one or two of the amateur barmen and waiters at the fete last week. Messrs. H. Greenall and W. P. lands showed uncommon aptitude, and metaphorically speaking, fortune lies at their feet. *_* *_ _ . •

Some of the get-rich-quick methods of the " grafters" and " boodlers " in San Francisco are described in another column by our old townsman, Mr. Henry Whitham. They make one thankful to be "poor proud, and particular" in jog-trotting old England.

Whitecross Ward has the highest birth-rate and the lowest death-rate in the town. It has a population in regular employment and mostly getting good wages, save Dr. Gaffikin in her first annual report as Deputy Medical Officer. * * *

We note that the Sanitary Works Committee of the Corporation has purchased a horse from Mr. P. Davies of Warburton for :CS!. Were tenders obtained by advertising in this case, according to standing orders? An ;£Bl horse for some of the purposes of the Sanitary Works Committee seems rather a luxury. * * *

Mr. H. P. Hughes' departure from Warrington will be regretted by many for ?yen those who have differed from him most on political grounds have been among the first to recognize his kindliness and more particularly his unselfish interest in young men who are just making a start in lffe. We wish him well in his new venture in his native land. *

The Borough Surveyor- has been instructed to prepare specifications for a ferro-concrete bridge to replace Warrington Bridge, and also for a new bridge over the river at Howley. Hurrah, we're moving again ! But a lot of water has run under the old bridge since it was first condemned by the Council, and still it stands where it stood. * * *

We are sure our reader; will cordially join in the good wishes which have been so freely extended to the Rev. C. L. Stowe on his departure from the Warring- ton circuit. This young and energetic minister has made a host of friends during his stay amongst us and has earned the high regard of a largo circle outside his own denomination.

* * * TO THE MONTH

We've prayed for the warmth of the sunshine And sung you a suppliant tune, But you have vouchsafed never one shine, And June, you have left us je jtine. —" The World."

* * Cockney Tommy: Say, Jimmy, ow, cliyer spell wt?

Jimmy: F-o•u-g-11-t. Cockney Tommy : Not that one—the other. Jimmy: F-o-r-t. Cockney Tommy : I don't mean the fawt yer live in ; I mead the fawt yer fink wiv.—" Tatler." ;! There is no prospect of the extension of the tram vays to Bruche Bridge and Padgate Bridge. The extended time allowed for the construction of these works will shortly expire, and the Committee has decided not to apply for a further extension of time, the tramways manager having reported that to carry the tramways to these two points would involve a considerable financial loss.

* * * The Watch Committee has resolved that the pro- prietors of heavy road locomotives passing through the town be charged a fee of 2s. 6d. per day. What about the heavy motors which start from Warrington? They cause most of the nuisance, but we suppose they will escape having to pay 2s. Gd. every time they turn out. We should be better pleased if all and sundry had to pay about .Z 2 6s.for breaking the peace and the roads. * * *

As an example of humorous Parliamentary deliberation, the following would be hard to beat: Captain Craig asked in Parliament on Monday for what reason a flag-pole was erected on the new War Office.

Mr. Haldane: For the purpose of flying a flag. (Laughter.) Viscount Tumour : Can the right hon. gentleman say on what occasion it is proposed to fly a flag ? (Laughter.) Mr. Haldane : (Laughter.)

Oa appropriate occasions.

** * I

As a result of a request from Stockton Heath, the Corporation is going to apply for a provisional order to supply the following townships with electric power for lighting purposes :—Penketh, Great Sankey, Orford, Winwick, Fearnhead, Padgate, Latchford Without, Thelwall, Grappenhall. Lymm, Stockton Heath, Appleton, Lower Walton, Higher Walton. We would just caution the inhabitants of these villages (begging Stockton Heath's pardon) against premature dreams of electric illumination. A provisional order is only a very short step along the " path of light." * * *

The balance sheet of the Tramways Undertaking for the year ended March 31st last has been presented to the Committee, and shows a net profit of :£1309 17s. 10d. on the year's working. It will be remem- bered that at a recent Council meeting the manager's request for an increase of salary was refused until this report had been presented. The Committee now recommends that he receive a further .7e25 per annum, which will make his salary _CI7S —not an extravagant sum by any means! t, _ _ _

The public, through the Executive Government, are by far the biggest employers of labour in the country. The Army, Navy, Civil Service, Post-Office, and so on account in round numbers for say 1,000,000 persons whose wages are paid by the State. Now the State is you and I and the rest of us, a piece of machinery to do our work, so that, getting down to bedrock, we are responsible for this crowd ; responsible if they are ill-paid, ill-treated ; responsible also if, on the other hand, they do not give a fair return for the money they are paid. —"London Opinion."

Saturday was a fateful day at Hill Cliff e, and the takings at the banar raised the proceeds from mediocrity to splendid success. On Friday night the sum realised only amounted to £1,500, just half of what was required to meet the debt on the Infirmary extension, so that it was evident Saturday'must set up a record if even a reasonable measure of success was to be assured. Fortunately the last day proved equal to the demand, and by the time the proceedings closed another thousand pounds had been poured into the lap of the treasurer. The last " lap " was the fastest! Deducting expenses, there will be over :£2,000 clear for the Extension Fund. which, considering that the weather was frequently very trying (and trying hard to be fine) is eminently satisfactory.

WARRINGTON

Town Council. The monthly meeting of the Town Council will be held on Tuesday evening at 6 o'clock at the Town Hall. Recent Examination Results. In announcing the result of the Union of Lanca- shire and Cheshire Institutes' examinations the success of Mr. Fred Ashcroft in the German examination was stated to have been in the elementary grade. This should have been the intermediate grade.

Personal. Sir Gilbert Greenall and Lord Yarborough received the King at the Royal Show on Wednesday. The Royal pavilion had been sumptuously furnished under the direction of Lady Greenall. ' The Mayor of Warrington was amongst those who attended the proceedings on Saturday in connection with the New Brighton promenade extension. He was also one of the guests at the dinner, which was subse- k.uently held in the Tower Grounds.

"LE-FRANCIS," "PREMIER," and " RUDGE- WHITWORTH " CICLES. Price from £4 10s. Cash or easy payments.—J. S. GARLAND, 51 to 55, Bridge-street, Warrington.—Advt. Bold-street Church. The new organ at Bold-street Wesleyan Church will be opened on July 11th when a recital will be given by Dr. J. Kendrick Pyne, of Manchester. Special services will be held the following Sunday at which recitals will be ,given by Mr. W. H. Payton, Mus. Bac. Full particulars of other special musical services will be found in our advertising columns. Compensation for Injury. At the County Court on Thursday one of the actions down for hearing was that in which Frederick Brooks, a boy living in Amelia-street, sued the Firth Co., Ltd., Florence Mills, for damages under the Employers' Liability Act for injuries sustained by his hand being caught in a revolving block' whilst following his employment on January 23rd.—Mr. Cuthbert Smith (instructed by Mr. Steel) appeared for the plaintiff, and Mr. Acton, barrister (instructed by Mr. E. A. Connor) for the defendants.—Mr. Smith said the parties had come to an arrangement. The company, although they denied plaintiff's contention that the accident was through their fault, were sorry for the boy and had agreed to pay him :VA and costs. That terminated what might have been a long and trouble- some case Volunteer Encampment. - - - -

It will no doubt be a source of satisfaction to War- rington Volunteers to hear that the annual encampment will take place at Llanberis, North Wales, during August Bank Holiday week. The battalion forms part of the South Lancashire Brigade, numbering 2,500. Officers of the brigade have inspected the ground and reported favourably upon the advantages which it presents. Efforts have been made for several years by the Carnarvon Town Council to secure the presence of Volunte4rs in the district for their annual training and much satisfaction is felt that their enterprise is to be rewarded. The ground is fifty acres in extent, but, through the kindnesq of Mr. Assheton- Smith, a large expanse of land on the slopes of Snowdon will be also available for manoeuvring pnrposes.

Hams 62d. per lb.—AsHLEy's, Sankey st.—Advt. A Year's Profit on Electricity. _

The annual report of the Co.:Poration electrical supply undertaking, published this week, shows that the net profit on the year's working was £2516 195.41. as against £2763 19s. 2d. the previous year. The decrease is accounted for by the re-assessment of the property by which the annual payment in rent, rates., &c., goes up from £3lB to £923. There has during the year been an increase in the consumptiog of current, the number of customers having risen from 285 to 320. The average price for current 1•52 per unit is notable, as only two Corporations change less and of these, one makes a loss of over £6,000 per annum, and the other does not show so large a net profit as this department. The plant is reported to be in a state of high efficiency. Wanted to Plead the Gaming Act.

At the County Court on Thursday, before his Honour Judge Shireas Will, LC., Michaff Connolly, commission agent, Leigh, sued Henry Jackson, 13, I Chorley-street, Warrington for A:22 money advanced. Mr. Browne appeared for the plaintiff and without stating particulars of the case, produced a letter in which defendant admitted owing the money but said he was not in a position to pay it at that time—Later it transpired that the money was claimed in respect of a betting transaction —Defendant said he wished to plead the Gaming Act and also denied making a bet with Connolly—Mr. Browne said defendant must give five days' notice before he could plead the Gaming Act and he had neglected to do so.— His Honour (to defendant) : I am afraid you have not much defence on the merits. Do you want an adjournment to plead the Gaming Act? Defendant: Yes.—His Honour: Then you shall have it.-31r. Browne pointed out that if defendant had read his summons he would have known that notice w'as required.He contended that defendant was not entitled to an adjournment.—His Honour said plaintiff went into the box but did not give any particulars of the claim, and it turned out to be a gaming transaction. Ile did not think that was the proper way to put the case before the Court and he would allow an adjourn- ment to the next Court day, costs being reserved,

ST. HELENS.

ST. HELENS STREET ACCIDENTS.

John Parry. aged thirty-six, of Bickershaw-lane, Abram, near Wigan, met with a serious accident at St. Helens on Saturday night. Parry had cycled to Liver- pool with a friend, and on returning was riding out of Sutton-road into Peasley Cross-land. Just as he reached the tram track he encountered a horse and cart and a boy ran across. I n turning sharply round to avoid the lad Parry was struck by an approaching tram- car and knocked forward on the track. The lifeguard picked him up or he would have been killed. When extricated, Parry complained of internal injury. Dr. Cooke attended, and on examination at the St. Helens Hospital it was found that Parry's back was seriously hurt.—Alhert Thomas, a boy of fourteen, of 94, North- road, St. Helens was caught by a motor-car on Sunday afternoon when he was crossing the road near the Lingholme Hotel. The car, which was driven by Mr. George Faulkner, of Granville Mill, Walkden, knocked the lad down. He was immediately pinked up by Mr. Faulkner and taken to Dr. Dow's surgery, the injuries consisting of contusions to the head and face and shock. SUDDEN DEATH IN ST. HELENS BATHS.

A very sad incident disturbed the bathers at the St. Helens Corporation Baths on Saturday evening, when Joseph Stott, aged eighteen, printer's apprentice, of 6, Bond-street, Present, died very suddenly. It is reported that the youth was extremely fond of swimming, but the exercise did not agree with him, for on three occasions he had fainted and had to be lifted out of the bath. Shortly before five o'clock on Saturday after- noon he dived in the deep end and swam to the steps. A minute or two afterwards a youth who was with him noticed that he had fainted, and that he seemed to be slipping off the steps. Assistance was at once rendered and Dr. Green, of ,Prescot, who happened to be in the baths, endeavoured to restore respiration, but in vain, death being due to a sudden attack of syncope.—Mr. S. Brighouse held the inquest at St. Helens Town Hall on Tuesday.—Deceased's mother said that on Saturday afternoon, when he said be was going to the baths, she begged him not to go, but he laughed and said she was silly. He had alwsys been healthy, and latterly he had been training to run at some sports.—Henry Hall said he had been with deceased on three occasions when he had fainted at the baths. He once tainted in the water. and witness had to pull him out.—r. Green, medical officer of Prescot, said the cause of death was syncope. There was nothing in the suggestion of drowning. When witness heard Hall cry out he immediately ran to deceased. Stott had stopped breathing, but his heart was faintly beating. He performed artificial respira- tion and tried to stimulate the heart's action, but the youth only made three very ineffectual attempts at respiration and died. He knew him very well. Deceased had an hereditary weakness, and since he had been training for running he thought his heart had become dilated. This might not have troubled him under ordinary circumstances, but the exertion of swimming had brought on an attack of syncope. He suggested that some brandy and blankets should be kept in the baths ready for emergencies like that.—The Coroner agreed with this suggestion, and a verdict of " Death from syncope, caused by over-exertion in swimming," was returned. CYCLIST KILLED AT ST. HELENS.

The inquiry into the death of John Parry, collier, aged thirty-six, of 10S, Bickershaw-lane, Abram, Wigan, who had his spine fractured in a tram accident on Satur- day, was held at St. Helens on Tuesday by Mr. Brighouse. —Mrs. Parry, of Clvvtybout, Cwmyglo. R. 5.0., Carnar- vonshire, identified deceased as her husband. They had not lived together for seven years, but she saw him at Easter.—John Barnes, collier, of Abram, said he went with deceased by bicycle on Saturday to Liverpool, and returned to Parr to see a friend. They found he had moved to Thatto Heath, and went by way of Peasley Cross to go there. When they got to the top of Peasley Cross-lane, deceased, riding first, got a the tram lines, and his bicycle being caught he was thrown under the tram. Deceased was a steady, s•ber ran-William Gee, of Sutton-road, and a Mrs. Latham spoke to seeing the accident. They looked at the men particularly because they each had toy balloons flying from their handlebars. The tram was going very slowly.—Edward Gilchrist the tramdriver, said he pulled up a few yards from the corner because there was a horse and van, and the horse was unattended and inclined to be restive. He than went on slowly to the stop of Peasley Cross-lane. The cyclist came staggering on as though ke had lost con- trol of the machine. - His feet were not on the pedals, I and one of them struck the tram. He fell under the tram headfirst.—lnspector Wallace said deceased made a statement to himself and Dr. Cooke. He said :—" I had come up the hill. When I got to the end of the 1, road I saw a horse and cart. I was going to pass it just ' as I saw a youth coming, and in trying to get out of his way I ran into the foot of the tramcar and carp to grief myself. I did not see the tramcar ; neither did I

hear him sound his gong."—Dr. Cooke 'mid the man's spine was fractured near the neck, and he was completely paralysed.—The jury returned a verdict of" Accidental death," and attached no blame to the driver of the car

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON

CONFIRMATION SERVICE.

A confirmation service was held at St. John's Parish Church, Earlestown on Monday evening, when 90 candidates presented themselves for confirmation. There were present Bishop Chavasse, the Vicar (the Rev. T. R. Matthews), and the Rev. A. Ainley, curate of St. John's. The Bishop addressed the candidates in a very impressive manner, both before and after the confirmation. There was a very large attendance. FARM FIRE AT BURTONWOOD.

An alarming fire broke out on Monday about noon in the centre of the range of farm buildings at I3rockhead Farm, Burtonwood,belonging to Mr. illiam Falrclough. A cyclist who was immediately despatched for the St. Helens Fire Brigade covered the six miles in very fast time. A four-stalled stable and straw loft had by that time been almost consumed, and all the remaining lonildiabr-s were in great danger. Superintendent Lyon utilised the water from a pond in the neighbourhood, and the fire was quickly cut off front tbe!other buildings and by three o'clock all was safe. The fire is supposed to have originated in the straw loft. The damage was about £l5O, and is covered by assurance. OBITUARY.

The death is announced of Mr. Philip Chandley, of Cross-lane. Earlestown. lie had been in the employ of the London and North-Western Waggon Works for over 40 years as cashier, and was also secretary for the Earlestown Building Society. The funeral took place on Saturday of Ernest William Mullins, of 19, Rathbone street, Earlestown, Corporal of the lst Volunteer Battalion South Lancashire Regiment. A large number of Volunteers attended the funeral, under the command of. Lieutenant Picton, Warrington and Earlestown being largely represented. Three volleys was fired at the graveside, followed by the beating of the drums and sounding of the bugles. The Rev. R. Trelartd, vicar of llaydock, conducted the service at the graveside, having had the deceased under his care while in Haydock Hospital, in which place he died, after a very painful illness. Much sympathy is felt for the relatives of the deceased. A great crowd assembled at th• Newton-in-Makerfield Cemetery to witness the last rites. The coffin, enveloped in the Union Jack, was carried to the grsve by four brother Volunteers, followed by four brothers, and other relations and friends, the deceased being held in great esteem in the neighbourhood. He was 27 years of age MR. SEDDON, M.P., AND THE HUSSARS.

In the course of a letter to the " Earlestown Guardian" Mr. J. A. Seddon, M.P., says note the political screech in your issue of June 14th, from the Tory agent for Newton. I :via not much concerned with the sickly assortment of bluff, sycophancy, and Uriah Heap-like sympathy for other people's pleasure for one day in the year. The writer says: 'The troops were giving their services freely and voluntarily, and were in no way acting under orders to appear, as Mr. Seddon in his letter foolishly suggests. Nor do the mon in any way desire or need his pro- tection." This, sir, is an impudent fabrication I consulted the War Office at the request of one con- cerned, who has too much respect for his Majesty's service to sea it degraded by political hucksters. If the Tory party are anxious for the truth I will gladly petition the War Office to undertake, under Yeomanry regulations, a full enquiry, whereby the name of the officer responsible shall be made public. If they want publicity they can have it. A grievance that rests upon a grave breach of military discipline and an insult to the King, can find little support with political hirelings. But who is it that wants publicity ? I note the officer responsible is discreetly silent ; the ex-member for Newton is dumb; a silence that can be felt has fallen upon the Tory newspaper. Way, it did not even print the choice epithets spoken by the Tory agent, but left it to mere Radical organs to enlighten the world with the wisdom of this oracle. Fie upon such baseless ingratitude! Oft from this quarter have I had loud- sounding blows—harmless in effect—foe causing con- siderably less consternation in the Tory camp. Why this conspiracy of silence? Can it be that the military gentlemen concerned have been soliloquising? "It was ft grevious fault, and grievously hath Citaar answered it." The chastened and silent spirit displayed is an eloquent testimony that the way of the trans- gressor is hard (when found out).

EXTRAORDINARY EMPLOYMENT.

Working in a Hot Stove.

Strange occupations and working under uncommon conditions are many, but perhaps one of the queerest is that followed by Mr. Sam Bowen, whose duties com- pel him to work in a hot stove. Mr. Rowen, who lives 1 at 67, Wellington-street, Keighley, Yorks.. gives the following interesting account of a marvellous cure by ' Dr. Cassell's Tablets, of acute inflammation of the Kidneys, complicated by fits, brought on by catching colds whilst engaged on his weird duties. Mr. Rowen writes :—" For the last 12 years I suffered a martyrdom from inflammation of kidneys and fits, which was brought on by getting cold after working in a hot stove I could not sleep or eat, and wasted away to a mere shadow, and could only walk with the aid of a stick. I tried every possible remedy, and was under seven doctors, who all told me my case was-a mystery. also went into four infirmaries, and was turned out of all incurable.

"I was in this apparently hopeless condition when I I began to take Dr. Cassell's Tablets. T found myself I jmproving after the first bottle, and now after taking four, I feel strong and well, and am able to walk about :10 miles a day without any fatigue." Surely this striking evidence will convince all, in whatever condition of life, that Dr. Cassell's Tablets are it unique remedy. They will restore and invigorate the most shattered constitution, will .build healthy flesh at the rate of 11b. per week, and are a certain cure in all -diseases arising from physical and nerve exhaustion. Dr. Cassell's Tablets are as safe and sure as they are pure and effective. PLEASANT PARAGRAPHS.

ONLY A CABIN BOY.

A big battle was being fought between the English and Dutch navy. Sir John Narborough was the English admiral, and the masts of his ship had been shot away almost directly the fighting began. In spite of the greatest care and the most splendid bravery, Sir John saw that the English sailors must be beaten unless he could get help. There were a few ships some distance off to the right, but they were to act as a reserve, and would not enter into the battle without a message from him. Sir John stood a moment, and wondered how that message could be sent. It was not possible to signal; there was only one way— the message must be carried. Sir John wrote his order, telling the captain of the reserve t come and help him at once; then he called aloud for anyone who was willing to be the messenger. Think of the scene a moment, and then you will understand a little what a brave heart was needed to carry that note. Below was the sea; ;above, around, in it there rained a heavy shower of bullets. The long swim would be trying enough, but to swim with the chance of being shot every second was terrible. Yet Many sailors came forward at their admiral's call, ready to risk their lives for their country's good. They were all grown-up men, and they must have stared in wonder as one of the cabih boys, Cloudesley- Shovel by name, stood up among. them. "Why, what can you do, my fearless lad ? " said the admiral kindly. "I can swim, sir; and If I -am shot I shall be missed legs than anyone else." After a moment's hesitation the paper was handed to the boy, who put it between his teeth and sprang overboard. How the men cheered him, and watched him as long as he could be seen! He -reached the reserve ships in safety, and as they went into action at once a victory was gained by .the English. When the sun was setting, Cloudesley Shovel .stood once more on the deck of the admiral's ship, and received his heartiest thanks. "I shall live to see you have a flag-ship of ydur own," he said. And the words came true for the brave cabin boy became Sir Cloudesley Shovel, one of the greatest :of British admirals.

A CLOSE RACE.

The sun was bright, the sky was blue, It was so fair a day, The wind sprang up, drew in his breath, -And cried, "Come out and play ! "

The little waves cried," Wait for us Before the sport begins! " Then tied their little white caps fast Beneath their dimpled chins.

44 Now," cried the wind, "we'll have a race, And see, who first can reach, By running fast and running far, The line of yellow beach ! "

'The little waves ran hand in hand, To win was all their mind; With steady step and eager breath The wind ran close behind.

" Oh," cried the waves, "the race is ours! We're first to reacethe land!" 4' Ho!" laughed the wind, then pushed them all. Down on the yellow sand.

The little waves eared not a hit, But laughed aloud in glee, 'Then smoothed their rumpled dresses out And scampered back to sea,/

ST. MICHAEL'S CHAIR.

St. Michael's Mount in Cornwall is crowned -with a church tower, and on the top of this tower there is a strange structure in /stone called St. Michael's Chair. No doubt in bygone days the chair was really a beacon, in which a fire was kept burning of nights to warn sailors off from a dangerous coast. But the chair was said to serve .another purpose, for it was celebrated for the mastery that it conferred on husband and wife. Whichever happened to be fortunate enough to secure a seat in it first was to rule in the house- hold.

GRANDPAPA'S STORY.

"Want to know' what that is, my boy; why, it is the tail of a fox—a brush 'they call it—arpd that one came off the 'cutest rascal I ever saw or heard ,of."--" Oh, grandpapa, do tell me about it. I know there is a story, and I do so want to hear it !" ex- claimed little Fred Lester, as he watched his grand- futlior cleaning his gun.

"You do, eh? Well, lad, so you shall, and then you will know Why people so often say, as cunning .as a fox.' It was when your father was a boy, and we were living on the old farm at Felton. I Was very proud of my poultry, and took great care of them; but one night, in spite of my precautions, the foxes entered the yard and carried off four of my choicest geese. I was very angry about this and determined that, living or dead,' I would catch the rogues. I killed some geese purposely, and, poisoning them, left them lying in the yard; but though I repeated the experiment, and always -found by the snow that a fox had been prowling .around, the geese were never touched. Puzzled by this, I resolved to investigate matters for myself, :and coming one•day across the dead body of a deer I thus arranged proceedings. It was a bright moonlight night, and placing the dead deer fully poisoned a little way outside the farmyard, I took my stand behind a clump of bushes, and, gun in hand, patiently awaited results. I had not waited very long before I heard in the distance the un- .mistakable hark of a fox. Nearer, nearer, it came, until I could plainly see the animal creeping over the snow. Cautiously he crept up to the deer and ;sniffed at it. walked_round and, round it, and nail,-

Best Starch—best ironing.

sat .krfra cruse nynts side and gravely surveyed My finger was on the trigger of my gun, but the animals movements were so strange I forbore to shoot it, being curious to see what it would do next. For nearly ten minutes the fox sat there looking at the deer; then, raising his head, he gave cent to a series of howls, as if to say, no go; Lien don't leave dead deer about for any good purpose, and getting up, he prepared to trot oil, but a bullet from my gun stretched him lifeless on the snow." " Oh, grandpa! " cried Fred; "the poor fox, it was too bad to kill him." "I daresay, but, you see, it was 'life or death 'for my poor poultry, but I must say he was a clever scamp." "I should think he was," laughed Fred. "Thank you, grandpa, for your jolly story."

A CURIOUS RACE.

In the north island of Japan and in some: parts of tho,extreme north-east..of Asia there lives an in- terestiag race orpeopie called Ainek. Where they came from nobody seems to know, for in many res- pects they are not at al l like the Japanese and Chinese. The men are well built but rather under-sized, and there bodies are covered more or less thickly with hair. They wear long beards, and the hair of their head is also long and bushy. The women are not so well looking, as they have to do all the hard work of the tribe. The folk are not gifted with much intelligence., and they worship natural objects and animals, the bear being specially selected for this purpose. They spend their thne in fishing and hunting. It is hard to tell what is the number of the Ainos. But as they are not supposed to number more than 50,000 at the very most, it is likely that they will sooner or later become extinct.

You would not have though it, Her gown was so faded and plain; "Bound out "for her food and a shelter, And toiling in sun and in rain ; Running her errands so quickly, Milking the cows in the lane; Yet happy and sweet as the morning, " Without no thought that she might complain. "How is it," I asked this sweet maiden, "That you can so cheerily sing; That service so faithful and loving You are ever so ready to bring ? " She looked in my face for a moment, And a light from her eyes seemed to spring; "'Why should I not joy ? " she made answer, "For I am the child of a King.' "

ABOUT THE SLOTH.

In the dense forests of Brazil and other parts of South America there lives a curious hairy animal. Its movements are so sluggish that the first travellers who saw it nicknamed it the sloth. But just as the sea-lion ashore is clumsy and in its native element graceful, so the sloth when it takes to the trees shows considerable activity. Feeding on the leaves, buds, and young branches, and always finding plenty of food at hand, this creature is not remarkable for intelligence. Its chief enemies are huge snakes, against which it uses its powerful fore legs and claws effectively. The sloth passes from tree to tree back downwards, and when a gap occurs in the forest it has cunning enough to strip the tree upon which it is resting of every particle of foliage before it descends.

Said Mr. C.:—"ln those days I lived in Boston I was a Republican, and my friend R., who was the most penurious man in New Eng- land, was a Democrat. One day he said to me, C., have your committee been after you?' 'Not yet,' I teplied. 'We'll, mine have been after me, and they wanted me to subscribe 500 dollars, and I told them I would,' said he. I looked at him in amazement. Then he added, Yes. I told them the Republicans would expect 500 dollars from you, and I was going to pair off! So mind you stick to that arrangfment. It will be all the same to both parties, and it won't cost either of us a cent."

During a weekly kit inspection in an infantry regiment, stationed at a large town, a mop had been accidentally left propped. with the head uppermost, against a spare bed-cot. The cap- tain of the company, who was said to be very short sighted, on entering the room, pointed to the head of the mop, and exclaimed in a loud, sharp voice: "Colour-sergeant, see that man fiets his hair cut." The colour-sergeant, taking in the situation, remarked: "Very good, sir," and, smartly calling out "Attention !" to stop the laughter of the men, paseed on to the next inspection.

A South African farmer had just received from England, among other farm utensils, a milking stool. This he gave, with strict in- junctions to use it, to a Kaffir whose duty it was to milk the cows. The next morning the Kaffir returned from the cowsheds bruised and battered, but with an empty pail. In answer to his master's demand for an explanation he sadly replied: "Him berry nice milk stool, but the cows won't sit on him ."

"What do you mean by that?" demanded the astonished official. "Well, I'm going to be a stoker in the Navy," responded the customer.

There it is, over the trees," said mamma. The little boy looked up and finally saw it; but he said: " 'Tain't lighted yet, anyhow."

The Examiner, Saturday, June 29, 1907.

THE VOICE OF LABOUR.

Edited by John Workman.

Readers of this column are invited to assist in making it interesting and helpful to social workers by contributing themselves.

Correspondents must write on one side of the paper only, and endeavour to make their letters and notes short and to the point. A nom-de-plume may be used, but the real name of the writer should be foewarded in confidence to the Editor of the column. J.S.S. must be addressed to John Workman, " Eveaminer " Office, Warrington and should be sent in not later than Wednesday in each week.

"NOT JANNOOK."

Sir, —I am penning these few lines to direct your attention to a matter which will, I think, be a little interesting to the Editor of "The Voice of Labour" column.

tieing somewhat of a studious nature, I frequently visit-the Public Reading Room in Bold-street. You will doubtless be aware that, in addition to the daily and weekly papers fixed on the stands running along two sides of the compartment, there are also a con- siderable number of magazines and other periodicals placed on the rack and counter within easy reach of every reading-room habitue. ;-trange to say the only spnarent exceptions to this rule are the two weeklies "The Labour News "and "The Labour Leader" both of which are generally I planted (and sometimes partially hidden) on a shelf behind the counter. Therefore anyone desirous of referring to either of these publications is obliged to first summon, from an adjoining department, an attendant to hand him the required journal. Let me make my Position quite quite clear, Sir. I have never yet read "The Labour News," whilst I hold no brief for " The Lab Dur Leader." In fact, I have no great liking for this Socialist organ, as its editor pre-supposes that the Snowdens, Glaziers, Bardies, and a little clique of I.L P.ers have a plete monopoly of the true social reform spirit in this country,

Siill, I contend that the members of the Libraries Committee must believe that there is a public demand for these particular papers which should, in my opinion, be placed in equal prominence with the other periodicals in a Public Reading Room. Hoping you will pay an early visit to the Bold-street Library and judge the case for yourself, I remain yours &c.,

"FAIR PLAY."

"TIME IS MONEY."

A few thousand people and myself visited the Infirmary Bazaar and Fête last week. Among other things I saw an amusing little comedy chiefly remark- able for the characteristically English way the only hero muddled through to his desired haven. He had my sympathy, for I remember coming home from holiday once with 11d. in my pocket, and the cabby waited while I sought and found in an egg-cup in the cupboard the jubilee shilling I had been saving for a rainy day. Is Time Money? It depends upon the point of view. For instance, time is simply a pittance to the sufferers from home work who receive 2d. for making boy's knickers, 4d. to 9d. for met:i's coats, sd. to Bd. for trousers.

But time is money to the Rockefellers of the world who receive a few pound sterling every minute. Is time money to the poor widow with two young children who was found making shirts at Bd. per dozen and had to ind her own cotton? Her earnings (save the mark) were 58. to Bs. per week. Plenty of time for precious little money. But time is money to some folks. In 1888 some of the leases on the Portman estate expired and were only renewed on extravagant terms. The tenants had to pay enormous sums for the privilege of renewing the leases. One tenant had her rent raised from 10 guineas to £BO, paid a lump sum of £l,OOO to the landlord, had to spend £5OO on the house, paid all insurance premiums and the lawyer's bill for drafting the agTeement. Lord Portman is stated to have made (I mean netted) a profit of £1,250,000 by this and other similar deals. He would require a rather large portmanteau to carry it away.

- Time is money. Pourteen -years doing nothing, yot the money rolls in. like a flood-tide with a strong south-westerly gale a'blowing. Is time money to the homeworker making corsets at 101 d. a dozen? A: woman gets Is. 9d. at it in three days. Time so spent is far from being money, but the only remedy that appears to be known to our rulers is the appointment of another commission to prove the obvious, to demonstrate the already plain. I am afraid we shall have to find the remedy ourselves and bring it to the notice of the powers that be.

LAZA RUS.

RELIGION IN THE SCHOOLS.

Labour men believe in education both for themselves and their children. They are tired to death of the religious controversies associated with primary teach- ing in England and Wales. Religion should be left to those who believe in it and are willing to pay for It. At this time of day it is an anachronism to teach religion in the people's schools. Tile Warrington Education Authority obviously believes in compulsion at any cost. It has introduced and carried through a religious syllabus, which it intends having taught in all the Council schools. Why? One says you can't have morality without religion. Well but morality is religion, if not what is it? The man who is most truly moral is most truly religious, while the man who is religious in the generally accepted use of that term may be one of the biggest scoundrels in creation, Jawn D. for example. Another objector says you will soon have the country wicked and immoral, and the children grow- ing up without any knowledge of God and Jesus, and all the rest of it. Well the country is wicked and immoral enough as it is. It certainly could not he worse if we had no religion at all in the schools. My own opinion is that the churches are strong enough, if they were only willing and eager as well, to get hold of all the children in the Kingdom and give them all they need, to kelp them to grow up good men and women, an honour to their country, and the churches and themselves.

At any rate it's getting too late in the day to think that the nation will much longer put up with the pre- sent system. It is unfair to the teachers, to Ale child, and to the State.

What do we want? First a repl downright good system of instruction in literature, science, art and mathematics, with plenty of open air exercise for the children. These must be adapted to their ages and capacities.

Then special treatment for the feeble, the weak and the backward child. He is an asset to be reckoned with on the national balance sheet. If necessary he should he fed, clothed, shod and his general health attended to. We are not our own entirely. We belong to one another. They recognise this in America, at any rate, to some extent. _

Moreover a child's teachers must be well-trained and well-paid. So we must have national training Colleges over the portals of which must be carved in letters large enough for the most shortsighted priest to read "PAWS OFF.? Here time must be given for the mind and heart and imagination of the future child- instructor to expand. Good schools, plenty of them, gond teachers and small.classes. Thorough and well-grounded teaching, not cramming as was the custom years ago. The foundations of future culture should be laid whether the child intends td be a mechanic or a college pro- fessor. Ignorance is fatal tp the true welfare of a country. The best pedagogy must be in friendship with Ole Laboar men want the best schools money can provide and they mean to have them, SAM WELLER, <Tux.

TIIE MUNIC,IPALIZATION OF THE DRINK / TRADE.

STANLEY LORD, REPLIES TO HIS CRITTS. note the contributions of Messrs. Griffiths and O'Brien to the correspondence columns of the " Examiner." I have ambled over the arid acreage of their acidulated animadversions and have plucked the few solitary shrubs' of sense from the few oases of argument and to. them I would reply.

It often happens that one's Critics are mutually destructive and this case is no exception, for, whilst Mr. O'Brien says my opening remarks are "not a, good example of philosophic temperance' of language." Mr. Griffiths admits the truth of them. Mr. O'Brien gives us a very eloquent statement of the objects of the 1.0.G.T. which takes up much valuable space and is scarcely germane to the issue. Ile also takes me to task for my remark that the terms sensible men and Socialists are synonomous. Had Mr. O'Brien been a Scotsman I could have under- stood such obtuseness, but if I may infer his nationality from his name I am surprised that a quick witted.son of" John Bull's other Island" did not at once see the facetiousness of such a remark.

Mr. O'Brien also resents being called a fanatic, a

Nobody told me the public house was the working- man's club. Having eyes to see I speak what I know. Again I assert it is so aid t us in spite of Mr.O'Brien's assertion that to every man who makes the public house a rendezvous there are twenty who do not. Were this true there wouldn't be muck of a drink problem to solve.

Also nobody tnld me that Temperance men claimed for themselves all the virtues. I have hoard thent do it so often and I still think it is this pharisaical fanaticism that constitutes the stumbling block to real temperance. Mr. O'Brien says that drink sold by a parson is no better than that cold by a publican. Who told him so?

Thi's is important. ',never said a parson ought not to be entruatsd with the sal. of drink, but I do say that with public management our drinks would be purer and consequently less injurious whilst the manager would have no inducement to push the sale of bulk, and the patrons of the "pub" would not feel constrained to drink r frequently for " the good of the house."

The public-houses of Warrington are mostly squalid arid drinking is the only diversion in many of them, and in spite of police supervision etc., drunkenness is of regular occurrence. With public-houses belong- ing to the community there would be a social super- vision that wonld prevent drunkenness. This will answer Mr. Griffith.,.

Mr. O'Brien objects that under municipalization he would be a shareholder in a trade he deplores and would like to destroy, but he must recognise that as a citizen he to-day benefits by the revenue derived from the drink trade and many things must be done in the name of the community in future to which the indi- vidual unit may object, but individual objections must be made svbservient to the general good. Mr. Griffiths hits below the belt when he sneeringly supposes that Socialists would become off-licence holders, and again when he says municipalization would be profitable to the Socialist section of the community, and lastly when he talks of the glar- ing spirit of greed and avarice at the base of the pro- posal. Mr Griffiths and others of his kidney would benefit as much as any Socialist from municipalisa- tion, and he surely must know this. Socialists are not animated by the spirit of greed or avarice. They desire the well-being of the community as much .as anyone, and they are prepared to work and to make sacrifices to attain such an end. They would serve the cause of true temperance by removing the evil conditions that drive people to drink, by improving the public-houses until they became more like the cafés and beer gardens we hope for in the future, and less like the dirty dens for the distribution of drink they are to-day. Socialists do not. however, propose to abolish drink or to interfere with the liberty of the individual in the question. They realise that drink is desired by most people, that its supply is largely regulated by hue and profitable Trace, and that its sale is surrounded by conditions that make for excess. They say that by the public engaging in the trade in their corporate capacity the conditions that make for excess can be removed, the revenue can be need for public purposes, many of which, by improv- ing the environment of the people, would make for temperance.

The pity is that temperance people in their desire to organize the majority tyranny of prohibition neglect the means that lie nearest to their hand and by classing the moderate drinker with the drunkard they hinder instead of helping the cause of temperance. Municipalization as a solution is no worse because it was once advocated by Mr. Chamberlain and I com- mend its careful study to " the man in the street " who decides elections.

The now Workers' Compensation Act which comes into force on July Ist claims attention in every station of life. The liabilities of employers for accidents to their servants will be greatly increased ; workers will benefit, but the effect of the new law will be that only those in vigorous health will retain their places or be selected for vacancies. A sickly or anomie man or woman is more liable to accidents and the accidents are more serious in effect, so that employers will not engage weaklings. MY DEAR NEPHEWS AND NIECES,— . Here I am back again at my desk after a splendid time "down by the sea." I expelt little later, when your school breaks up, you will, most of you, be off for your annual trip, and will be enjoying yourselves with spade and pail by the seaside, or with lovely rambles and long walks in the country. Now this suggests to my mind a competition in which you can all take part. . . What kind of holiday do you like best—one by the sea or one in the delight- ful country? I know some of my readers who just love to go off to the sea coast, where they can splash and paddle to their heart's con- tent, but I have some more nephews and nieces who always tell me that they enjoy,. themselves much better in the country, a:'nd they chatter, away until their poor old Uncle is almost tired out with their wonderful stories of the fine romps and rambles they have had away in the country, at some quiet farm or wayside cot- tage. Now, then, which do YOIJ like best? For the beet two essays of not more than 100 words, I will give two 'splendid prizes. Send in, if possible, before next Saturday. It will not take you long, and if you don't get one of the first prizes, I may send you a consolation prize of some kind. Well, I shall wait and see if you deserve it.

Now I am ever so pleased at the letters I have received lately, but there are some of you from whom I have never heard once since you joined the corner. Fancy that! Of course, it is only because you have forgotten or been too busy, but remember, I shall expect .a line this week, so just sit down and write it. I want you all to read "Peaches and Patches." it is a delightful story sent me from across the seas for this column.

With love to you all, Ever your affectionate UNCLE NED.

CROSFIELD'S SPORTS.

Successful Gathering at Warrington.

The fifth snarls' sports in connection with Messrs. Crosfleld's Recreation Club were held on the Football ground, Wilderspool. on Saturday. The weather was fine, but cold, and the attendance was not so large as usual. The different items on the programme were all confined to the workpeople and valuable prizes were offered. There were a large number of competitors and the racing generally was good. During the pro- ceedings a number of selections were given by the Work's hand and these were much enjoyed. Results :

WHITE CITY, MANCHESTER.

The opening of the White City in ITanchester marks an epoch in outdoor amusement enterprises. And not to be outdone the Management has started its ball rolling with an almost endless variety of new and novel attractions for those who journey there looking for diversion and outdoor amusement. The former Royal Botanical Gardens are now transformed into a veritable Sylvan retreat, which makes it a distinctly. new feature. Not alone during the afternoon where delighted visitors listen to the playing of the best bands in Europe, but also at night is it a most beautiful spot .with its 40,000 electric lights set in artistic arches. The bands no doubt will prove one of the great features of the City of Pleasuse, which has been aptly termed "One of the earth's most beautiful spots, and not unlike the iluxurious palm-land of Bermuda." Messrs. Heathcote have demonstrated their artistic temperament in pro- ' diming the most beautiful ever seen in England. It is something more than that which has ever been seen before. It is the most gigantic yet attempted any- where in the amusement line. The White City has made ample provision for Mothers and Children, not alone in devices and attractions which will appeal to them especially, but where they can rest and enjoy themselves. It is proposed to cater to outings, and every provision is made for parties at reduced rates. Mr. J. Calvin Brown, the Managing Director of the White City, having Made outdoor amusements a study for many years will no doubt sustain his irreproachable reputa- tion he has he has done in America.

PROPER PRIDE.

Children are very sansitive about the appearance of the little packages of fowl brought from home to be eaten on the school premises in the presence of other children. They like to have food that not only is nice but looks it. Thinly cut slices of Currant Bread and butter amply fulfil both these conditions and form a light sustaining lunch that can be eaten with pride and profit by the most fastidious of little folks.

Shooting Match at Appleton.

The non-commissioned officers of the Ist. V.R. P.W.V. South Lancashire Regiment, recently indulged in a shooting match on the Appleton Rifle Range. PENKETH.

I BABY'S REMARKABLE ESCAPE.—A boy about 18 months old, named Frank Tomkinson, living at 49, ! Warrington-road, bed a very narrow escape from serious injury last week. Whilo playing in the roadway ran towards a cart standing opposite Brookfield-terrace, I snd the driver, not noticing the lad, started the horse, when the wheel of the cart passed over the child's side. ,He was taken with all speed to Dr. Murray, who examined him, and fortunateiy found that no bones , whre broken. He is, however, suffering from shock and ! bruises.

GLTILD.—The members and friends of the Penketh and Padgate Wesley Guilds silent an enjoyable afternoon on Saturday when they visited Frodsham. From 20 to 10 went on cycles and the remainder by train from Fiddler's Ferry, meeting the Pedgate friends at Warrington. The party visited the Frodshsm branch of Dr. Stevenson's children's home and Orphanage and reached home at about ten o'clock.

GROWN-UP LAND.

"Good morning, fair maid with eyelashes

brown, Can you tell me the way to Womanhood Town?" " Ohl This way, and that way, and never atop; 'Tis picking up stitches grandma will drop; 'Tis kissing the baby's troubles away; 'Tis learning that cross words will never pay; 'Tis helping mother; 'tis sewing up rents; 'Tis reading and playing; 'tis saving the pence; 'Tis loving and smiling; forgetting to frown— Oh! That is the way to WOMANHOOD TOWN." (Sent by A. TATTERSALL.)

THE BLANK BOOK.

An old man and a golden-haired boy sat to- gether. A book lay before them. Its binding was bright and new, -ite, pages clean and white, but blank. "This book is yours," said the old man, "and each page representaa day. It is for you so to write in it, that the book, when full, may not be less fair than it is now with its leaves white and spotleFe." The 'boy took the gift joyfully, confident that it would be better for his handiwork.. A year passed away. The old man called for the book. The child came slowly with hanging head, and gave it up reluctantly. Every page was defaced with crooked lines and ugly blots. "Can, I not rub them out ?" said the boy tearfully. The old man shook his head. "Marks made on these pages cannot be rubbed out," said he, ".hey must always remain. But try again; see, Were is another book." The child looked up and Sighed. I cannot write well," he said, "unless you guide my hand." A book is set. up before each of you every day, and you have to fill its pages. What will you write in it? Unkind thoughts, selfish words? You certainly will Unless you aek God Who gives you the day to guide your hand. (Sent by HILDA TATTERS ILL.)

.ESA (BY CAROLYN WELLS.. It was Dorothy's birthday, and she was seven years old for the first time in her life.

She had had beautiful presents. Mamma had given her a silver thimble with D on it, and papa had given her a love4r drawing-slate. Aunt Edith had sent her a book, and grandma a dear little box of handkerchiefs. ,

But the biggest gift of all had come by express from Aunt Jennie.

And it was'nothing more nor less than a pink gingham dress with a pocket in it! In all her seven years ,Dorothy had never had a pocket before, and she begged to be allowed to wear the new dress to echool, that all the girls might see the wonderful pocket. "But you can take a holiday to-day if you like," said her mother, "and stay at home from school because it is your birthday." "No, mamma," said Dorothy. "I want to go to school speshly to-day; and I want to carry my patches in my pocket. And, oh. can't I have a piece of the ' sky ' ? " "Yes," said her mother, smiling, "as it is your birthday, you may have a piece of the 'sky.'" You see, Dorothy's little school was kept by two dear, old-fashioned ladies, who taught sew- ing as well as other lessons. And the beginners in the sewing class always made patchwork. And Dorothy was a beginner. Every day she took four neatly cut pieces of silk, and came home with them all sewed together in a lovely block for her quilt. Her mother cut the pieces for her from differ- ent coloured silks, and, of course, some were prettier than others. But loveliest of all was a yard of light blue satin which Aunt Jennie had sent for this very purpose. It was such a fair, clear blue, that Dorothy called it her "sky," and was always glad to sew a block cut from it. So on her birthday she happily folded the carefully cut pieces of sky in a bit of white tissue paper, and deposited the parcel in her convenient new pocket. Her new thimble also went in, and one of grandma's new handker- Chiefs. Then the happy little maiden kissed her mother and ran off to school, which was only three blocks away. As she went out of the gate she met the grocer's man coming in.

"It's my birthday!" she said, for she was well acquainted with him.

" Arrah, it is?" he said. "Thin I'll be afther givin' ye a present. Here's two peaches for ye. They're not big, but they're ripe and sweet, an' they'll do ye no harrum." 'Dorothy thanked the good-natured man, and, putting the peaches in her new pocket, compla- cently thought how many nice friends she seemed to have.

Stopping to talk to the grocer nearly made her late for school, but by hurrying a little she reached there just in time to march upstairs in the line.

Then came singing and other opening exer- cises, and at half-past nine the sewing class was called.

"It's my birthday," said Dorothy to Miss Katherine, "and I have a lovely blue-sky block to sew."

"That's nice," said the teacher. "Let me see

Dorothy dived down into lier pocket, but quickly pulled back her hand in dismay. You see, the peaches were very ripe, and as Dorothy was not in, the habit of sitting very still, but often wiggled about, and occasionally bumped against a desk or chair or the little girl next to her, those peaches had just smashed them- selves into a jelly, and you can imagine what the sky-blue satin bits looked like Dorothy tried not to cry, but she was natur- ally a tidy little girl, and the stained, sticky blocks and peach-filled pocket just seemed as if they were going to spoil her whole birthday. But Miss Katherine said kindly, "Oh, what a sad accident! But never mind, deary, you can be excused from sewing to-day." "I don't mind so much about the block," mill Dorothy, still bravely fighting back her tears, " but my new pocket is so—so horrid!" Then what do you think Miss Katherine did? She just took out her scissors, and ripped out that little pocket. and took it away into another room. And she threw away the soft peaches, and washed and ironed the pocket and the handkerchief, and. rescued the little silver thimble, and then she sewed the pocket in Dorothy's frock again, and the sun shone once more. But Dorothy learned a lesson never to put peaches and patches in the same pocket.

THE MATERIALS WERE HERS.°

Ethel's father is an artist. The New York Pre.gs says he often permits his little daughter to stay in the studio with him and paint. Ethel has some brushes and paint of her own, and usce them with some ability and more satisfac- tion. One day a visitor called at the studio, and Ethel eagerly showed him a small canvas. "rid you paint this?" asked the visitor, in surprise.'

"Yes, sir," said Ethel. "Well, well, it is very good indeed! Are you cure you painted it all yourself ?" "No, not all," Ethel admitted, reluctantly. "Papa helped me a little,"

"Oh!" "But he used my brushes," the little girl hastily added.

A GOOD CHILDREN'S MEDICINE.

Probably nothing tests a woman's patience more than restless cbildren. In the spring and summer months, when nature is forcing the kidneys to do extra heavy work, and the natural channels are not ready to take i away the impurities thrown out by the blood, children I are tldgetty and fretful. Their skin becomes cloißEy, land irritating pimples and spots are troublesome. Remember-this, and instead of scolding, try a course of • the following prescription,which is said to clear the skin in a week, and will help the kidneys to carry away the poisons which have collected in the systems during the winter: Tincture of Buchu, one ounce ; Karox Compound, one ounce ; Syrup of Lemon, two ounces. Any good chemist keeps these ingredients, and will put up the prescription, or it may be mixed at home. Half it teaspoonful should be taken after meals and at bedtime for a week and the result noted. A professor at Lehigh University has made a calculation to shew that if a tiny vessel of one cubic centimetre (0061 cubic inch) capacity is filled with hydrogen _corpuscles there can be placed the i -rein, n round num- ber, 525 octillions 525,000,000,000,000, 000,000,000,000,000—0 f them.
