CONCRETE FOR SHIP SALVAGE.

An ingenious plan is described in Coin- mercial America- for using concrete for sal- vage purposes in the case of the wreck of the George W. Elder, sunk in the Columbia River and badly holed ,by rock. One hole, 80ft. from the bow, measured 35ft. in each direction. Bulkheads were built by divers forward and aft of the break, and two more aft of the engine-rogm, heavy canvas was placed over the rock which projected into the ship, And concrete was placed over the canvas until a thick covering over the, entire surface had been formed. When the con- crete had set it waS held down by a beam placed athwart the hold. The water was then pumped out and the vessel floated, and was taken forty miles to a dry dock. -

===== 0002601_19070629_art0123.txt | included=true | included =====
THE COLOURS OF NATURE.

Nothing in nature is actually the colour that we see it, says a- swell-known scientific observer. It only appears to U 6 at a given moment as a particular colour in relation to other apparent colours which surround it. Thus we may walk out .on a rainy evening when the sky and everything was grey, and come indoors and light the lamp, and im- mediately the sky which we .see through the window appears as a beautiful and tender blue, though there was no tiace of blue in the sky a minute before, when we were out- side. The change ,is produced in our 'senses by the colour of the sky taking its place in relation to a range of warm colours in the lighted room. In the Same way the presence of a mit.n with a lantern, or a light in a win- dow, will apparently change the colour of things in its neighbourhood, and • a mass of any strong colour, such as red, blue, or orange, will supest its complementary colour in surrounding objects.

===== 0002601_19070629_art0124.txt | included=true | included =====
ONLY ONE WHEEL IN A CLOCK.

A clock has ben made to frun and to keep time by means of a single wheel, which is nat a gear wheel, but _simply a perforated disc, so that the timepiece might aptly be called a. gearless clock. The work ordinarily performed by a pendulum and a gearing is done by a quarter-inch steel ball, which rolls over two inclined plates in just a minute of time and rolls off the lower 'plate into the lower hole of the disc, it the same time re- leasing the disc, which' is always under ten- sion imparted to it by two ball' weights sus- pended in towers on either \side of the dial. The disc carries thirty balls on one side, and

when released by the rolling of the ball on the lower plates against a locking device turns the space of one hole, or one minute, thus bringing the uppermost ball into position to roll on the top plate and .begin its zig-zag course down the two inclined plates, as did the preceding ball. Each ball rolls over the plate once ev.gry ,thirty minutes.

===== 0002601_19070629_art0125.txt | included=true | included =====
FATIGUE OF METALS.

Why does a barber clip his razor hitt, hot water before beginning operations? This was one of the questions answered by Dr. Ewing in, the Wilde lecture to the Manchester Literary and PhilosOphical Society. The custom, it appears, is not a mere fetish, nor •is it practised in order to sterilise the blade, though it has a scientific justification. In the process of stropping the particles' of metal forming the cutting edge are swayed to And fro, and forced to take positions other than those which make for stability and per- manence, or, to use the technical expression, " fatigue " sets in. Now recovery from fatigue amongst metals, as among more im- portant aggregations of molecules, is as- sisted by warmth and rest, so that the effect of raising the temperature is to allow the particles to set themselves in a manner satisfying their mutual affinities. What goes on during the heating, then, is a hurried re- arrangement of particles • and an adjustment of polarities favourable to the keenness of the edge. It was not stated whether a twenty- foul hours' rest, such as would be secured if the razor were stropped immediately after instead of before use, would have the same effect, but the matter hardly admits of doubt. Most razor manufacturers recom- mend stropping after Use, but, as they do not explain why, the advice is frequently overlooked.-

===== 0002601_19070629_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
FATIGUED BY LIGHT.

Further experiments confirm the earlier observations, following Hertz's discoveries, on the " fatigue " induced in metals by the continued action of the ultra-violet rays of light. This fatigue affects the capacity of metals for photoelectric effects. Mr. H. Stanley Allen, in studying the fatigue of zile, has observed that the itpplication of the longer light-waves brings about a certain amount of recovery of photoelectric activity. These results, although primarily of tech- nical interest only, possess practical importance.

===== 0002601_19070629_art0127.txt | included=true | included =====
DEFLOCCULATED GRAPHITE.

Deflocculated graphite is the invention, says a writer in the Scientific American, of E. G. Acheson, the discoverer, of the pro- cesses for making carborundum and artifi- cial or manufactured graphite. In the de- flocculated condition produced by Mr. Ache- son, graphite has a condition of fineness far beyond that . attainable by mechanical means. In fact, its condition resembles, if not wholly approaches, the Molecular state. The "effect," for such it must be termed, is .pro- duced with water and a comparatively small quantity of gallotannie - acid, and when thus treated the unctuous graphite remains sus- pended in the water, shewing not the slightest disposition to .settle. The black liquid passes with case through the finest fil- ter paper. Severe tests have demonstrated that it is an admirable „lubricant. There is every reason to believe that deflocculated graphite, with or without oil, will become a popular agent for all classes of lubrication, for, strange as it may seem, deflocculated graphite possesses the remarkable power of preventing rust or corrosion of iron or steel. It has been known for years that graphite is au excellent lubricating body, both in a dry state, and particularly when associated with oil, fats, or water., Strenuous efforts, ex- tending over a long time, have been made to suspend graphite in a liquid to •be used as a lubricant, but all these efforts were unsuc- cessful. It is a well-recognised fact among scientific men that plain water has many ad- vantages as a lubricant if it had sufficient body to withstand the pressures brought to bear, and to which lubricants are subretecl. Its very high speeific heat would be of great advantage to keep down the temperatures of bearings, while its low viscosity .would re- duce • friction, bid unfortunately it has not sufficient body to withstand the pressure of an ordinary bearing. It also has the fatal quality of rusting and corroding metals, thus making., it absolutely worthless as a lubricant. The " effect " which Mr. Acheson has dis- covered makes it possible not only to reduce graphite practically to the molecular state, and to cause it to remain suspended in water for an indefinite neriod of time. but also to prevent iron and steel from rusting or cor- roding while associated with water, thus ren- dering of advantage the high specific heat "ad low viscosity of water.

REV C. L. STOWE'S FAREWELL

The Church and the Masses.

The Rev. C. L. Stowe concicded his ministry in Warrington on Sunday when he peached his farewell sermons at Legh-street Primitive Methodist Church. On Monday he left Warrington for a brief holiday prior to entering upon his duties in the Eccles Circuit. Addressing the Sunday school children in the after- noon Mr. STOWE said he thought they ought to have a larger number of scholars; they should be content with no fewer than 250 at least. Their staff of teachers should be greater, for the problem of a successful Sunday school rested with the teacher. They, ought further to have better order than obtained at present, punctuality should be more stringently practised, and then their school would become more perfect; in fact a model of what a school should be. There was a crowded attendance at the evening service when Mr. towe took for his text the words : "The path of the just is as the shining light, that shineth more and more unto the perfect day" (Proverbs iv., 18). He said these words showed that. there was always something to look for- ward to. Ihe best of their life was in front of them and not behind. The Christian experience was to be like the rising sun. They were not to go from more to less,but from less to moretfrom the first streak of sunrise to the full blaze of day. Each period of their life should be grander than the one which preceded it. Youth should be better than childhood, maturity better than youth, and old age in some respects best of all The youth should know his Saviour better than the boy. The man should have ce.nvictions more deeply rooted than the youth; and the aged one should be able to give the noblest testimony of all. Tley must not think that they were to put a full stop anywhere in their life so far as their Christian experience was concerned. God wanted their death to be more of a meridian than a sunset. In their own Church enterprise they ought to look for progression, advance, growing influence. There was something wrong when the population of a town Increased by leaps and bounds and sot much more Church accommodation was required. Should they have shops,roore banks, more railway stations,and no increase in the attendance at Church? Business men were not satis- fied until they cosild add store to store, field to field, dividend to,dividend ; and Churchmen should not be satisfied unless an increasing number of men and women were brought within their influence. It was to be feared the Church had not been enterprising enough in the matter of reaching the people, and so he wanted to plead for a snore active propaganda,a bolder programme, a more faithful living out of the Gospel of Jesus. If they would have an experience like the rising sun, it would not do to be talking only about the good old times. They ranst believe that" the best is yet to be," and they had every reason to believe it, if only they were faithful in their work. 'They must adapt them- selves to changing circumstances. There should be a more determined effort to arouse the interest of the masses. By great open-air gatherings, by persistent andiloving visitation,by the mighty power of.prayer,and by a baptism of the Holy Spirit upon themselves and all their activities, they might be used by God to create a spiritual concern among the people, which would make the Church the most interest- ing place in the community. They should rally round their own minister, whoever he might be, as much as they rallied round a special Evangelist. Mr. Stowe concluded by. applying the text to the progress of Christianity in the world. Nought could stay . the advance of the Christian Church. It was impossible to quench the growing lustre of Christ's kingdom, Individual churches might be unfaithtul, but the great Christian Church would spread from more to more and the gates of hell should not prevail atminst it. The choir feelingly sang the anthem "As the hart pants," and after the Benediction the congregation rose and sang the hymn" God be with you till we meet again." Mr. Stowe, uho appeared much touched, thanked the congregation for their kind wishes. The Sacrament was afterwards observed. Mr. Stowe' i successor is the Rev. J. W. Buglass, of Whitehaven, who thirty years ago had charge of the Warrington Circuit.

===== 0002601_19070629_art0137.txt | included=true | included =====
Mr_ Parker and the S.D.F.

Reply to Mr. H. Quelch

Sir,—Replying to the letter of Mr. H. Quelch, Editor of "Justice," I stand by what I said in the notes on my visit to Plymouth. At the meeting of the S.D.F. called to " unmask" the Liberal party, Mr. Quelch dis- tinctly stated that the Liberal Government had done nothing since they came into office, and this without qualification. He was so understood by the audieme, and so reported in the Plymouth press. Mr. Quelch in his verbal reply to Mr. Barnes, and in his letter in your paper, now suggests vatious qualifications to his bald statement. For example, speaking of two of the things the Government have done, the passing of the Trades Disputes and Compensation Acts, he tells us that the vital clause of the former was passed in consequence of pressure from the Labour Party, and in regard to the Workmen's Compensation Act that this was over-due. It is not necessary for me to dispute either of these points' because they are not pertinent to the issue. What Mr. Barnes challenged, and what I challenged, was the statement that the Liberal Government had "done nothina " to fulfil their promises to their sup- porters. It is unnecessary to labour the personal point, it only concerns the way in which Mr. Quelch by his loose and inaccurate statements tries to disparage the

work of the Liberal Government. Let us pass on to what is of more importance in the letter of Mr. Quelch, namely his defence of his assump- tion "that the Socialist party is justified in claiming to speak for the whole of the working-classes." He tells us that the Socialist party alone stands for the ! emancipation of the working-classes, the abolition of , the last form of human slavery—wage slavery—through socialization of the means of the production. He goes on to tell us that the Socialist party aims at an entire transformation of the conditions of the workers, and adds "this is why, although there are many among the working clsss who still vote for the old parties which would perpetuate their slavery . . . we are perfectly justified in claiming for the Socialist party that it speaks for the whole working class." I fail to see that the inference drawn from the premises follows as a logical sequence. The fact that a number of people are of opinion that a certain transformation of social conditions would be beneficial to the workers in no way gives them the title to speak for the whole of the working classes, Suppose that Mr. Quelch should devise some other form of social transformation, and form a party called Quelchites to advocate his scheme, would he have any right to say that he represented the whole of the working classes simply because in his opinion his method was better than any other? The majority of the workers in the country do not con- sider themselves "slaves " and have no faith in the Socialist remedy. Is it not therefore somewhat of an impertinence for the minority to claim that they can speak for the whole of the working classes? They have every right to speak for themselves, but not for those who differ from them. I took stock of the type of men who were sent up to the National Liberal Federation by the associations all over the country. Ido not suppose five per cent. of these men were of independent, means. They appeared to represent various classes of the country's workers. There were men from the forge, the loom, the shop; every phase of the nation's toil was represented in the delegates of this so-called " Capitalist Party." Does Mr. Quelch claim that his party can speak and think for I these men better than they can for themselves? I Is it not time this spirit of arrogance was put aside? Is it not time that the section of the patty to which Mr. Quelch belongs abandoned this silly talk of "unmasking " other political and social workers, this strutting about, and posing as the only sincere party, declaring"they are the people,"and that wisdom will die with them? Would it not be well if instead of doing their best to prejudice their own cause by being unjust ' to others, they were to try and convince those who do not see eye to eye with them? Are we not justified in asking Socialists to spare a little time and thought from their work of general vituperation and destruction and tell us what they mean to erect on the ruins of the social system they desire to destroy, or if they like the word better, to transform? The country is getting tired of the reiteration of the statement about "Nationalising the means of produc- tion." We must know a great deal of what this involves before we agree to take this leap in the dark. We must know for example on what system work is to be paid for. We have various proposals before us. Saint-Simon and others have suggested that the distribu- tion should be made in proportion to liability, or what appears to me a better word, capacity. Karl Marx repudiates this idea, and would assess all work, what- ever the amount or the character of the product, as an equal value, making time the only measure, and award- ing accordingly. Louis Blanc and others consider that neither the time worked, the quality, nor quantity of the work ought to govern the reward, but that every man should have according to his needs. There are other proposals, but they mostly group themselves round these three. We have a right to know before we adopt the first proposal, how society will be able to ensure that every man gives according to his abilities, and how it is to compel this contribution if it be with- held. If the second principle is to be favoured we want to know if the man who works an hour, and does a certain amount, is to be paid the same as the man who works the same time but does twice as much. We want to know if the third plan is adopted who is to determine the extent of the wants or needs of the various individuals. I have frequently raised these difficulties in the many friendly talks I have had with Socialists, and the replies I have got have been something like this :"These are details which will settle themselves when we have got everything nationalized." But these so called details go to the very root of the social question; they- are more important than the framework of the system itself. Until Socialists can show that in connection with the proposed mechanism of society the individual's share in the things that minister to his life will be apportioned on a principle which will appeal to the units of the state as just, they have no right to ask society to adopt their scheme. Apart altogether from the important questions of reward of toil, effort and ability, the transference of initiative and management, in the most important departments of life, from the individual to state officials,will, without doubt, restrain the free development of self-conscious personality and deaden the sense of personal responsibility. It is no answer to point out the imperfections incidental to the present system. Bacause monopolies ought to be state-owned or state-controlled, there is no reason why we should replace an easily resistible capitalism by an irresistible officialism—an officialism which will have to attempt the impossible task of equit- ably assigning to each man what work he must do, or determining his capacity to do it, and of justly assess- ing his share of the products either on the basis of the value of his contribution, or the extent of his needs. CHARLES E. PARKER. Penketh, Juno 25th 1907.

ACTION BY THE GUARDIANS.

At the Warrington Court on Thursday the Guardians of the Warrington Union sued Mary Ann Robinson, 39, Marsh-street, as administratrix of the estate of her late husband Harrison N. Robinson and also sued her per- sonally for the return of £l3 14s. 10d. paid in out-relief to defendant and her late hasband.—Mr. Browne appeared for the plaintiff and Mr. Steel defended.—Ur. Browne said defendant and her husband were the recipients of out relief. On the husband's death defendant became entitled to £lO9 due on an insurance policy and as under the Poor Law Amendment Act of 1834 out-relief was declared to be a loan,the Guardians contend that the ratepayers should not be put to the expense of the relief when she was in a position to pay. Every opportunity was given her to appear before the Board and make an offer but she would not do that—Mr. F. H. Aukland, relieving officer, cross-examined by Mr. Steel said defendant's husband was 79 years of age when he died and had been a ratepayer of Warrington the greater part of his life. He heard that defendant had to pay some money in respect of the policy,but did not know it was £5O. He heard that money had to be borrowed to keep up the premiums and that Mrs. Robi- nson repaid it out of the insurance money—Mr. B. Shearer also gave evidence—Mr. Steel I submitted that the Guardians had failed to make out their case as they could not differentiate between the amounts for which defendant was alleged to be liable personally and as adrninistratrix. He could prove that the whore estate had gone within a pound or two.—His Honour said he was of opinion that the out relief was a loan to the husband and therefore defendant was not liable personally. She was, however, liable as adrninistratrix and he gave judgment against her in that capacity with costs.

MR. CKOSFIE ID'S MAIDEN SPEECH.

THE HOUSE OF LORDS DEBATE.

Warrington's Member and "the Will of 06 People."

Mr. A. H. Crosfield made his maiden speech in the House of Comtnons on Wednesday night on the Prime Minister's resolution with regard to checking the veto of the House of Lords.

Received on rising with cordial cheers, the Hon. Member quoted Lord Byron's remark that there was one subject on which most people were eloquent, and very few agreeable, that was to say, themselves. (Laughter.) Still on the first occasion on which one rose to claim the indulgence of the House (Cheers)

A FEW PERSONAL OBSERVATIONS

' might perhaps be pardoned. He was delighted to have an opportunity of explaining why he was ventur- ing to make a few remarks, and why he intended to vote in support of the motion. He could claim no title to rush in where great constitutional authorities had evidently trod with circumspection and caution. ,He would have been content simply to subscribe to the course the Prime Minister had taken, because in his humble opinion one saw in the resolution the sagacity, and at the same/time, the courage and sincerity which had endeared the leader of the party to all who followed hirn,—(cheers)---including humble Members like himself, who, if he might say so, regarded their chief not merely with respect but with a feeling of affection. (Renewed cheers). And so he told his friends in the constituency he had the honour to represent, that while they had on those benches such an abundant flow of oratory, the Whips were quite content if they had his vote without too much of his voice. (Laughter.) At the same time he would not have his constituency think he was shirking a tight place. He had been into a crevasse on one occasion, and under an avalanche on another, and he could assure hon. Members who had not tried them that they were very tight places indeed. (Laughter.) After such an experience he would not shirk the ordeal still less would he miss the privilege of addressing the House on behalf of his c mstituency.

" I am very glad," Mr. Crosfield continued, "that I have an opportunity of speaking to-night, because this is an occasion which, after all, affects not a single section of one's constituency, but which concerns the whole of it. I know the views of the large Liberal and Lablur element which I have the honour chie fly to represent. I know their views, and the views of my Irish friends who live in Warrington, and I feel sure that so far as this motion carries us they will approve of the vote which I intend to give in support of it. Therefore, so far as argument may be neces- sary, I feel inclined to address myself in a rather special manner to the Free Trade Unionists who did me the honour to support me at the last election. I put it to them—so far as I have any grip of the funda- mental issue, and the pith and essence of what we are proposing—l put it to them that it amounts simply to this : that after due precautions against hasty legis- lation, and without undue and dangerous delay

THE WILL OF THE PEOPLE SHALL PREVAIL.

(Cheers). We have been told in this very interesting debate that distinguished Members of another Chamber profess to know that will as if they were a Chamber of Zancigs. lam afraid that does not hold water at all, and we have proof to the contrary of the most overwhelming kind. One of the best summaries of that proof we listened to in the speech of my friend, the Member for Clitheroe (Mr. Shackleton), who, in a very able speech, gave us a lucid and convincing summary of the measures which the Lords have rejected which were wanted urgently for the benefit of the people. (Hear, hear.) The case against them is I fear equally strong whether we con- sider what they have done, or what they have left undone. For when prejudice and passion and race hatred have run high have the Lords ever tried to use their undoubted influence in the country to allay these mischievous sentiments?

"May I refer, Mr. Caldwell, for a moment or two, to the speech of the hon. gentleman, the Member for Oxford University (Sir William Anson) and his con- tention that when the time came to ttanslate this motion into a Bill, we should have a single Chamber, and consequently bad and hasty work. lam sure the great majority here feel that quite the contrary is the danger, that we run the risk of too much delay, a point that was made very clear in the frank, powerful, and brilliant speech we heard this afternoon from Mr. Lloyd-George. He—and- last night the Under- Secretary for the Colonies said the same thing— pointed out that we could not legislate for the case of a mad people returning a mad Parliament. Neither can we, nor are we, going to waste time in anticipat- ing the impossible. (Hear, hear). Mr. Morley wrote a good many years ago that he had no great love for second Chambers, and although I dare say there are plenty of people in this country who think the consequences would be disastrous if we had no second Chamber, I for one should be able to sleep in my bed quite comfortably. I believe in the pru- dence and sense of fairness of the English people, and J. believe in the advantage of full responsibility being placed upon the representative Chamber. But I believe at the same time, that it is better to have a second Chamber if only because, for my part, I would not like to see the crown dragged into the dusty turmoil of party politics. (Hear, hear). That is not the only reason ; there are other reasons why it is better to have a second Chamber. But I am truly thankful that

THE GOVERNMENT MEANS BU,IENESS

on this question, and that in future we are not going to have the interminable delays,—and the emasulation and cutting down of our measures, delays which have told against the real interests of the nation for so long. (Hear, hear and cheers). The allusion has been made in this debate—and indeed the Prime Minister laid emphasis on the fact— that in their graver issues politics are something much more serious than a game. Yes, that is true, and so is life, but as the Right Hon. gentleman, the I Member for Montrose has reminded us, laughter has a !fore part in it. It would indeed be hard luck to deprive the poor politician of his little b:t of humour. lam told that one day Dan Leno came down to the House, and the Hon. Gentleman the lember for Woolwich—whose wit and genius is not second even to Leno's— (hear, hear) —met him when he was leaving and said, " Well, Dan, what do you think of it all ?" "Not bad ! " said Leno, "Not had ; but I think it would go better to the piano ! " (Loud laughter. Pogr—Leno. We all miss him—(general cheers), and all appreciate his little joke at our expense. If there is humonr in the House, there is also good humour, and I venture to think that any foreigner who has been present this week would consider this great debate with its far-reaching issues a striking example of it. And a new member making his maiden speech must of all others be most sensible of the kindness, and the generous courtesy of the House, for which I sincerely beg to thank Honourable Members. (Cheers.) NEW YORK, May tllst.—A fund of 1,500,000 dols. to be expended in fighting "Industrial oppression " in the next three years, was called for by President Van Cleave of the National Association of Manu- facturers at its annual convention to-day. President Van Cleave appointed a committee of thirty-five to find a way to raise the money. Mr. Van Cleave announced his plan at the con- clusion of his annual address, in the course of which he declared the principles of the association to be to maintain the open shop, to oppose the boy- cott, limitation of apprentices and limitation of output and to oppose dictation by labour unions. He also declared that the manufacturers must com- bat the newer issues caused by the determination of labour union leaders to terrorize the President, Congress, judges and juries. Mr. Van Cleave aeded "We wale, to federate the manufacturers of this country to effectively fight the industrial oppres- sion. We should provide ways and means to feder- ate the employers of the country and to educate our manufacturers to a proper sense of their own duty, patriotism and self-interest."

- The Boot and Shoe Workers' Union, Local 216, has passed a resolution imposing a fine of 25d015. on all members detected in patronizing street cars manned by strikebreakers. One-half this sum will be paid to the informer. Tailors' Union, No. 2, has also levied a 25-cent weekly assessment for the aid of the striking car- men, in addition to the donations made to the strik- ing laundry workers and telephone operators. A fine of 25d015. will be imposed on members riding on street cars during the strike, and 10dols. must bo paid for any such offence committed by any member of the family of a member. Eight new members were admitted. Janitors' Union, No. 10,367, has contributed 10dols. each to the striking Laundry Workers' Union and Telephone Operators' Union. A fine of 10dols. will be imposed on any member riding on a street car manned by non-union men, the fine to be paid to the striking carmen. The brewers and the beer waggon drivers have been on strike. I think to-morrow's paper will tell me it is settled. The drivers wanted 255. a day and shorter hours. I think they will get 245. and shorter hours. No need to be surprised at all these strikes. The men know that to a large extent their employers are thieves, and they go in for their share of the plunder. Now how is it going to end, labour or capital ? It is the beginning of the end to a system that is rotten from root to branch, and we shall be fortunate if the change be made without bloodshed. H. WHITHAII. May 27, 1907.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

Cricket.

Warrington are distinctly in the running for the championship of the South-West Lancashiie League, and are dangerous rivals of the leaders. On Saturday they inflicted a heavy defeat upon St. Helens Recs. after the most disastrous opening of the season. Batting first on a slow wicket Richardson and Wright were out with only seven runs on the board, and ten runs later Podmore and Callon had also returned to the pavilion. Connor and Bibby were brought together, and aftei a shaky opening, made a determined stand. Connor, who made his first innings of the season, was given a life early on, and then he proceeded to run up the score. Progressing largely by means of fours he quickly increased his total, and was past his half century when he stepped out to a ball from Harper, missed, and was smartly stumped. His innings was a typical one, and many were the hopes expressed that he will in the immediate future be seen more frequently in the field.

Bibby never appeared to he happy and scored but slowly but he kept his wicbet up and his innings was far more valuable than the actual total would make it appear. None of the other batsmen gave much trouble and St. Helens Rocs were set 124 to win.

It was evident from the commencement of their effort that they were not going to be successful. Titvo singles were taken off Creswell's first over and Pilkington faced Callon. The first ball he played very tamely whilst the second found his wickets and made way for Harper. Both bowlers sent down a maiden and then Callon uprooted Harper off stumps. The visitors could do nothing with the bowling aud one after another they were sent back, the innings closing for 44.

The bowlers divided the honours pretty evenly though Callon secured slightly the larger share. By the way the younger player was in a most erratic mood and the large number of extras was due to some of his deliveries being out of the reach of Duckworth. He, however, has one pet ball, a kind of half volley, which looks simple enough but which the St. Helen's men found altogether beyond them. He and Cresswell have done practically all the bowling of the season and the records of both are excellent. they are : Runs

Howard for the Wilderspool team set his men a good example in the batting line by running up 17 and ,also did considerable damage with the ball. A scratch eleven got together by Mr. Daintith succeeded in defeating St. Elphin's C.C. on Saturday. Batting first St. Elphin's ran up 46, out of which Locker (17) was top scorer and in reply the scratch team totalled 52. Essenhigh was the top scorer with 13 and also took seven wickets.

RUNCORN MAN MISSING.

It has been reported to the Runcorn police that Mr. Arthur Foster, of Ivy-street, has been missing from home since ruesday of last week. He has recently been employed as an insurance agent, and was well known in the town. He was last seen on the Transporter Bridge crossing to Widnes.

SAN FRANCISCO AND HER TROUBLES.

[By M. H. WIT ITHANI]

It is not an easy task to gather together the various and numerous troubles that nom, and have for a long time, troubled the city of San Francisco. In my opinion Boss-rule may be said to be the cause of these troubles, an yet Boss rule has its defenders even among those who are not seeking for gain by means of graft, or what in plain English should be called stealing. When I read the quotation given by John Workman from the sermon preached by the Rev. W. Bracecamp I thought, yes it is quite true, "On the Stock Exchange the Jew could discern no difference between the pro- fessing Christian and himself in a readiness to take an unfair advantage over another." For several years a Jew has been the boss of San Francisco, Abe Real by name. This man has been organizer and dictator of the municipal graft machine. Depending upon this man's silenCe,the bribe-giving magnates of the business and political world have pursued their course of corrup- tion. Graft entered into everything that goes towards making up the political,municipal and business life of a city. No line of money-making, legal or illegal, could ibe pursued without first greasing the palm of Reuf. Saloon or restaurant must shut up unless the jingle of gold was heard. Gambling dens and hotels alike, all contributed their share of dollars to the "machine." The proof that the street car line,through its President, paid Real 200,000 dols, to do as it wished with the city streets is now in the hands of the prosecution. Thousands of dollars were given by the prize fighting fraternity for permission to have a monopoly of the brutal business by means of a Trust. Presidents and lawyers of electrical, telephones, and gas and water companies, all have been in the graft business, and all I suppose, have been honourable men and will continue to be so, in the eyes of the world, being rich men. Months ago a prominent citizen took the situation in hand, and guaranteeing 100,000do1s. towards expenses, was able to provide two first-class men to aid him in running down the bribe giver as well as the bribe taken. The lawyer, Henry, graduated from the same college as Reuf ; the detective Burns has a national reputation. These two, working quietly, were at length able to apply for and obtain warrants for the arrest of Real, and then commenced a series of Court scenes that must simply have astounded the legal,, if not the lay world. It would take up all my space to begin to enumerate the ridiculous aspects of American law as seen in the prosecution of Real. It covered months, and each day the detention and keep of Reuf was costing the city not less than 20dols. a day. I may give one example of the preliminary tactics of the defendants. Great difficulty is experienced in gettinn•a jury together. The citizens fully,understand what they have to face when they undertake the duties of a grand juryman. For weeks these men were in the position of defendants. The prisoner's lawyers just badgered them out of their wits, and there was no •help for it. The Court could not interfere • each man's opinions were gone into, he was examined and cross- examined. Astute as the• prosecuting lawyer undoubtedly is, he had to wait and watch. His reward came ultimately, in the shape of a satisfactory jury, and the finding of a large number of indictments against Reuf, one of the ablest and most intellectual lawyers in While the Reufian comedy was going on, it is but a California.knedy after all, the Mayor had gone to Europe to look for points in his work of rebuilding the city. He had appointed as Deputy- Mayor a man of the name of Gallagher, by the way, it would be interesting to Englishmen to note the names of the army of American bribe-givers and takers. This Gallagher was a part of the machine, the said machine bein.g nearly the whole of the 'City's Council, and over the machine he presided. While the Mayor was away true bills were found against him. He is now out on bail. The Mayor, Schmitz, Reuf, and with I think two exceptions, the whole of the City Council, are now pointed with their faces towards the prison, and all, to a large extent, because Reuf could not stand the torture of the sweat rocim, but must con- fess, and bring the whole of the carefully built struc- ture' known as the "machine," down about his own and his colleagues' heads. Reuf stated that he received from, the Street Railroad Company the small sum of 200,000d015. Of this sum he gave to Schmitz 50,000d015, kept 50,000 for himself,and the remaining 100,000do1s. was divided among the City Council. The Corpora- tions are good payers. They can afford to give, their incomes being enormous. The San Francisco Street Rail-road Company does a great business in various cities of the Union. In Pittsburg it owns the Street Rail-road, and has an income of 50,000d015. a day. The indicted bribe giver,-Patrick Calhoun, is a multi- millionaire and is likely, along with Mayor Schmidt, Boss Reuf, and a few others, to wear stripes behind the prison bars. In all my writings to the " Examiner " I have rarely touched on the darker side of American life. I have not cared to, because the view I take of the situation is not by any means shared by the travelling public, and notably by the two Moseley Committees, and I might be charged with being prejudiced. I say distinctly that the educational system of America is had, decidedly bad. A few weeks ago a priest speaking on the situation said " a large majority of the Church-goers of San Francisco belonged to his church, and he said it with shame." For near upon 20 years I have worked with these men, I have heard their talk, and I don't know how anyone can expect the results to be other than what they are when the boast can openly be made that they have a clean slate to start with on Monday morning. Talk with the young men fresh from school and you will find that the moral side of their nature is entirely wanting. Of the ethical feature of life they simply know nothing. Dimes and dollars, is the greatest thing of all. I say distinctly that the one, thing America and the world has to fear to-day is no New Theology but the oldest theology of all, the one that makes a horror of death in the absence of the priest. This may possibly be a digression but as it is, in my opinion, at the root of the trouble I think it well to refer to it. As a Socialist I ought to have drawn in the Capital and Labour question, but I know this side of the trouble has no show so long as the cannot-dohwrong Church remains the power it is. The trial of Mayor Schmitz is being eagerly watched by the various indicted officials of the corporations owning public utilities, and they are banding them- selves together to give v 3. the Mayor their full support in every way. Millions of dollars will be spent to gain a victory for the Mayor. The prosecution know this and a large detective force is engaged to look after members of the Schmitz jury and see they are not tampered with during the trial. Now see the condi- tion this highly educated people are in. The Chief of Police has a number of indictments agaiest him. For a number of years this man has been practising his calling as chief protector of the public while he himself is out on bail, he being charged with protecting only brothels and gambling houses, and waxing rich in his efforts. They cannot move this man. If convicted they can- not send him to prison. The City Charter was drawn up by the boofflers and now it protects them. Even after conviction, according to the view of the prosecu- tion, Schmitz can continue in office until final judg- ment has been rendered by the supreme court. To oust the supervisors, therefore, would be to strengthen the Mayor's hands by enabling him to appoint their successors. From the statements of Spreckels it appears that San Francisco for the next seven months will be governed by a boodling board and a boodling Mayor. This man Spreckels is he who stands behind ; the prosecution. To leave the boodling business for ' a while let us look at the city. Poor San Francisco, how she has suffered through all this corruption ! The rotten work had started before the quake and the fire, now more than a year ago. One would have thought that the horrors of those ever to be remembered three days and nightsl would have been sufficient to turn these Church- goers to a better sense of their duties. I saw numbers ,of hastily erected altars in the streets with their attendant crowds of worshippers. I also heard, numbers of times, the man in the street Baling that it was God's way of punishing them for their sins. How soon that was all forgotten and looting began! I know of what I am speaking and I say that so soon as money, food, and clothing bagan to arrive in the city so soon did stealing begin. Not the least of our troubles has been the eternal labour question. Strikes without end, and I may say without reason We have now a street car strike on. The men want a three dollar pay for an eight hours day. We have now some 20,000 men walking the streets out on strike. The'chief demands seems to be a shorter day and more pay. A 1 the Unions seem to be gathering around the car men and giving help. The President, Calhoun, has this trouble to cope with, and the Courts are trying to send him to prison for bribery, so he has his bands 'full.

The truth of the matter is America is suffering because of her prosperity. The National Parliament and the various State Parliaments are run by the

LOCAL WEDDINGS.

Fairclough—Roberts. St. Matthew's Church, Stretton, was the scene of an exceedingly pretty wedding on Wednesday afternoon when Miss Ethel Carter Roberts only daughter of Mr. Henry Roberts, J.P., Kingsley, Hill Cliffe,Warrington, was married to Major Bwrereton Fairclough, son of Mr. Richard T. Fairclough, Barryfield, Latchford. Great interest was taken in the event and a large \con- gregation assembled. A striking scene was presented, the chancel being artistically decorated with palms and choice flowers. The Hon. and Rev. C. F. Cross, Vicar of Stretton, officiated. The bride, who was given away by her father, was attired in a beautiful dress of rich ivory chiffon moire, brocaded in small " Empire " design, trimmed with Brussels net, real Duchesse lace and silver tissue.She'wore an embroidered tulle veil with wreath of orange blossom and myrtle. The bridesmaids were Miss Silcock, Warrington, Miss Mollie and Miss Norah Fairclough, Barrvfield (sisters of the bridegroom), Miss Carter, Waterloo, and Miss May Roberts (cousins of the bride.) They were charmingly attired in white liberty muslin, the bodices being draped with kimono shaped fichus, edged with Valenciennes lace, and silver belts at the waist added greatly to the picturesque effect. They wore large leghorn picture hats trimmed with embroidered silk .net and pink lifalmaison carnations with touches of pale blue powder ribbon and they carried carnation bouquets tied with blue ribbon to match. Mr. Harry Fairclough (brother of the bridegroom) discharged the duties of best man. Mr. W. H. Payton, Mus. Bac., presided at the organ and played the following choice selections ;—Minuet d'Amour (Cowen), Salta d'Amour (Stgar), (a) Die Frage (b) Die Autwort (Wolstenholme), Lascia ch'is pianga (Handel), "My heart ever faithful" (Bach), Bridal March frcun " Lohengrin"( Wagner); Wedding March (Mendelssohn). The hymns" 0 Perfect Love and "Oh Father all. " were sung. As the happy couple left the church the bells rang out a merry peal. The recep- tion was held at Kingsley and a large number of guests attended. Later Mr. and Mrs. Brereton Fair- dough left for the South of England where the honey- moon will be spent. The bride's travelling costume was a fitting coat and skirt tailor made of a fine tweed in oyster grey. Many handsome presents were received including the following :—Workmen employed by Warrington Slate Co., Ltd., silver egg stand imscribed : "Presented to Ethel Carter Roberts by the workmen employed by the Warrington Slate Co., Ltd., on the occasion of her marriage to Mr. Brereton Fairclough, June 26th, 1907" ; office staff, travellers &c., of the Warrington Slate Co., Ltd., silver rose bowl suitably inscribed; employees of the Bank Quay and Mersey Mills in the employ of James Fairdough and Sons Ltd., books ; Colonel and officers of the Ist V.B. South Lancashire Regiment, silver revolving breakfast dish engraved with crest &c.

Hollingsworth—Hayward. There was a large attendance at Bewsey-road Wesleyan Chapel on Tuesday to witness the marriage of Miss -Winifred S. Hayward, eldest daughter of Mr. J. W. Hayward, J.P., 13, Wilson Patten Street, and Mr. Frank Hollingsworth, son of Mr. T. Hollingsworth 71, Lovely Lane. The Rev. J. Gunnell, Penketh, per- formed the ceremony. The bride wore a very pretty dress of white Ivory liberty satin trimmed with Brussels lace,and a wreath and fall. She carried a shower bouquet of white flowers, and also wore a sapphire and pearl brooch the gifts of the bridegroom. The bridesmaids were Miss Elsie Hayward (sister of the bride), and Miss Ethel Hollingsworth (sister of the bridegroolib and they wore cream cloth dresses strapped girth pale blue and silver, and tuScan hats with ostrich feathers, and carried bouquets of roses. IMr. Harry Knowles acted as best man. The organist (Mr. W. Pearce) played a number of selections includ- ing Mendelsshon's Wedding March and the _hymn "The voice that breathed o'er Eden" was sung. The wedding breakfast was served at the residence of the bride's father. The happy couple later left for London and the south, where the honeymoon is being spent. About 120 presents were received, including a hand- some silver tray from the directors and secretary of the Winmarleigh Cafes, Ltd., and a very pretty tea set from the staff.

Sephton—Eckersley. Much interest was occasioned by the marriage on Tuesday of Miss Bessie Eckersley, daughter of Mr. William Eckersley, New-road, Latchford, and Mr. John Sephton, Crow-lane, Newton-le-Willows, and there was a large attendance at St. James' Church to witness the ceremony which was performed by the Rev. H. B. Firth, assisted by the H. H. Corsbie. The bride, who was given away by her father, was becom- ingly attired in a brown cloth costume trimmed with cream and gold brocade. She carried a bouquet of white roses and lilies of the valley, with trailers of smilax and sweet peas. The bridesmaids were Miss Lizzie Eckersley (sister of the bride) and Miss Lizzie Winstanley (cousin of the bridegroom), and their dresses were of pastel blue tweed, trimmed with silk and velvet to match. They carried bouquets of sweet

peas. Mr. J. Sephton, brother of the bridegroom, acted as best man. A reception was held at Atkin- son's Cafe,Bridge-street, after which the happy couple left for Penmaenmetwr, where the honeymoon will be spent. A large number of handsome and costly presents were received.

MR. H. P. HUGHES' DEPARTURE. - Farewell 'lnner and Presentation.

There was an interesting and enjoyable function at Atkinson's CafO on Wednesday evening when a dinner was given in honour of Mr. H. Powell Hughes, who is severing his connection with the Richmond Gas Stove and Meter Co., Ltd., after nearly fifteen years' service as cashier and leaves Warrington for Wrexham to enter into business on his own account. Hr. Hughes is very highly esteemed by his fellow-workers and a large circle of other townspeople who have come into contact with him, and there was a general desire to give tangible expression to these cordial feelings. The matter was taken in hand by a committee consisting of heads of departments presided over by Mr. W. H. Sherburn, with Mr. J. Pollitt as secretary, and the outcome was that on Thursday Mr. Hughes was the recipiedt of a handsome parting gift in the shape of a purse of gold and an illuminated address. There was a large attend- ance st the dinner, Mr. Sherburn presiding. The spirit of the occasion was apprioriately expressed in the following lines "To a Comrade" which appeared on the menu card.

" Fellow-worker and friend, fare thee well, Youth perennial and bright be thy lot ; Though space may divide, recollections unite, True friendship is seldom forgot." An excellent repast was served, Hrs. Atkinson cater- ing in her best style. After dinner a highly enjoyable musical programme was submitted. Miss Rigby sang very sweetly " Love, the Pedlar," and "It was a dream," and was warmly applauded. Mr. F. Taylor was heard to advantage in" Asleep in the deep." and Mr. W. H. Tickell gave a spirited rendering of "The Wolf." Mr. Greenhalgh sang" Land of hope and glory" with fine effect, and later gave an equally successful interpre- tation of "The Veteran." Mr. D. Mackay delighted the assembly with a stirring recital of the immortal "Charge of the Light Brigade." Gramaphone selections on Mr. F. Sherburn's fine instrument proved a popular feature of the entertainment. Miss Sutton and Mr. Hazlehurst ably discharged the duties of accompanists. The event of the evening was, of course, the presenta- tion to Mr. Hughes.

The CwurtataN said the occasion was full of interest and he appreciated the honour which his colleagues had done him in asking him to he their mouthpiece. A gathering like that served to bring out various latent qualities in those who participated in it. For instance, it made them realise that in spite of the hurry and bustle of business,there was a brotherhood behind it all which sometimes got obscured if nothing but business stood before them.As fellow-workers they were tied 1)7, bonds much stronger and very much more beautiful than mere mercenary bonds. Those who knew him (Mr. Sherburn) intimately would know he had an ideal that there was a comradeship in work which was alionst above any other comradeship he knew of. Those present had tried to realize that ideal of the brotherhood of toil, and it was most desirable that business should be simply a larger specimen of family life. Mr. Sherburn proceeded to pay a high tribute to the late Mr. E. W. T. Richmond, who, he said, was revered by all who knew him. They were proud to bear the name of a gentleman who, however smart were his busi- ness abilities had great ideals apart from those and was thoroughly in earnest in trying to attain them. (Applause). When he (Mr. Sherburn) entered the service of the firm seven years ago one of the first persons with whom he became associated was their guest of that evening. (Applause). Anyone who had occasion to go to Mr. Hughes at the office of the firm would know how courteously they were always received (Applause). Mr. Hughes helped to make him (Mr. Sherburn) feel at home and ever since he had been as a brother to him and to a great many more. (Applause.) He would remain their brother, for although he was leaving Warrington they would not soon lose the remembrance of his kindly actions and they wished him well in his new sphere. (Applause.) When they heard that Mr. Hughes had decided to resign there was a spontaneous feeling that steps should be taken to show in a tangible form their respect and honour for him. (Applause.) That desire took shape by the heads of the departments consulting and forming a committee. The proposed presentation was put before Mr. Hughes' fellow workers, and as soon as it was heard of a number of friends in the town were desirous of being included in this token of respect. The matter was taken up enthusiastically. No pressure was needed, and that night he had to ask Mr. Hughes to accept as a token of their regard a purse containing £75 and a beautifully illuminated address which read as follows :—" Presented to Mr. H. Powell Hughes (Ap Howel) together with a purse of gold by his colleagues on the staff and fellow-workers in the employ of the Richmond Gas Stove and Meter Co. Ltd., Warrington, and a few friends, on the occasion of his resignation after nearly fifteen years' service with the Company. The subscribers desire to place on record their appreciation of his sterling qualities as a colleague full of sympathy and ever ready to do a service—especially when the need is greatest—and to encourage by word or deed the juniors coming within his influence. May the future in his new home be full of joy and happy usefulness as well as of real success and may it bring opportunities for development on lines of progress con- genial to his tastes. Signed on behalf of the subscribers John R. Burnett, John King, David Mackay, James Pollitt, W. Rigby, W. H. Smalley, Frederick Taylor, W. H. Tickell, W. H. Sherburn, chairman of the oommittee. Warrington 26th June 1907." The address was beauti- fully illuminated,and was embellished with the Borough Arms,a portrait of the late Mr. Richmond, and a picture of the molten iron issuing from a cupola. There were also representations of the Welsh Dragon and of the emblems of industry. Mr. Sherburn in handing the gifts to Mr. Hughes said he was sure he would cherish the good feeling which prompted them and that in his new home he would feel all the stronger for having the good wishes of those he had felt behind in Warrington. (Applause.) In acknowledging the gifts, Mr. HUGHES said ; There are a few occasions, in every man's life, when his feelings are too deeply touched •to admit of even an attempt at adequate expression. That is exactly my position to-night. To break with the associations of close upon 15 years, involves a mental strain which only those who have themselves had a somewhat similar experience can realise. In simple, homely, but heartfelt words,l ask you to accept the very grateful thanks of my wife and myself for these handsome gifts,—and—above all—for the kindly spirit of appreciation they reprent. If I may couple a final request—along with this wit w- iedgment—l would ask everyone here to support' my friend and successor (I had almost said, and my pupil), Mr. Tickell, with the same whole-hearted spirit of comradeship, which, far so long a period, has been ex- tended to myself. In coming over—at this point in my speech—to his chair, to take his hand, I wish him every good wish—in recognition of our unbroken friendship of nearly 15 years, and may it continue for long years more. (Applause.) It would be a further favour to me, as a Welshman, if you would permit me to say just seven words of thanks in the old language. It may be many years before you have on the Com- pany's staff another Welsh-speaking officer, bound to you by ties of long aquaintance, f.o, although you cannot understand what I am going to say, you will not mind me putting on record my acknowledgments in the rich colouring of my own language. "Pi3b diolchgarwch chwi, am eich caredigrwydd." And now, friends,— let me say.—not farewell, but an revoir ! (Applause.) When Mr. Hughes sat down, the CHAIRMAN (Mr. Sherburn) rose, and asked would Mr. Hughes please translate, for the benefit of the assembled company, the purport of the Welsh sentence just quoted. Mr. HUGHES said the Welsh words referred to, expressed in a form which could not perhaps be so comprehensively conveyed in English his thanks for their kindness to him.

Mr. DANEEr. K. R. of Liverpool, said he had known Mr. Hughes most intimately during the whole of the time he had been ie Warrington.He was sorry he would never see Nfr.Hughes again at Messrs.ltichmond's,for he had always experienced the greatest courtesy and kind- ness at his hands.He wished Mir. Hughes every happiness and success in his new career. (Applause). A vote of thanks was accorded to the Chairman for presiding, and to the artistes for their valuable services. A highly successful gathering concluded with the singing of the National Anthem.

WALKING DAY.

YESTERDAY'S SUCCESSFUL FESTIVAL.

Fine Weather.

Warrington kept high holiday yesterday, for it was Walking Day—the day dearer to the hearts of the children than any other. Whatever Cotllo3 or goes, the celebration of this festival is never lacking in whole-beartedness and enthusiasm, all being filled with the laudable desire to make the occasion a memorable and happy one in the lives of the youngsters. From time to time there have been sug- gestions that Walking Day should be merged into some other holiday, but to many people this would seem to be almost sacrilege, so that each year the festival is looked forward to with the same eagerness, and perhaps the more so because it is a purely local. holiday. Parents spare no trouble or expense in order that their children may figure in all the glory of fine raiment and it goes without saying that the little ones enjoy the procession, which to some might appear to be a tiring affair, and participate with zest in the romps in the fields. This week it has been hard to realise that Walking Day was so near at hand, or indeed that it was June at all. There was so little sunshine and so much cold and rain, that people began to quake lest the Walking Day of 1907 should be a complete failure. But on Thursday their spirits rose, for the sun shone once again and everything augured well for the following day until the , evening when there was again a change for the worse land showers damped the enthusiasm of the many who had been purchasing summer finery. However, to the jo3 of all, Walking Day opened most promisingly and genial sunshine cheered ths hearts of the children as they made their way to the schools. Later clouds gathered threateningly and during the day there were a few drops of rain, but on the whole the conditions were ideal for outdoor enjoyment—a fact upon which Warringtonians have every reason to congratulate themselves. _

Thh town wore quite a festive aspect, flags and bunting adding a touch of colour to the streets. The prettiest sight, however, was pre- sented at the Town Elan, where the decorations which were purchased for General Boiler's visit were again brought out and made a very striking display. Sus- pended from the flag staff on the roof of the building were long lines of streamers which fluttered gaily in the breeze, while the approaches to the Hall looked bright with Venetian masts and a profusion of streamers. The balcony was artistically draped with flags and various light materials. The Mayor and Mayoress (Alderman and Mrs. Smethurst) and members of the Council and their wives assembled on the Town Hall steps to witness the massing of the scholars on the lawn.

An animated scene was witnessed as the Church of England and Nonconformist schools comprising the main procession arrived in quick succession. One missed the familiar figure of Councillor J. G. Craik. who for many years has shared with Mr. R. T. Fairclough the task of marshalling the youthful hosts. Mr. Fairclough once again discharged the duties and was assisted by Mr. J. T. Locker. The police- arrangements were excellent, Chief Inspector Phillips being in command. With bands playing and banners flying, the scholars marched on to the lawn, and when all had taken their places, the effect was imposing. Promptly at ten o'clock the centre gates were opened and the Blue Coat School led the way in the march round the town. The procession was in the following order :

(11 Blue Coat School and Band. . (2) Paclgate Industrial School and Band.. (3) Parish Church and St. Helen's Church, Hollinfare,and Band of ate Bth and 40th Regimental District. (4) St. George's Mission and Pioneer Band. (5) St. Cletnent's Mission and Band of Newton Farm School. (6) St. Peter's School and Mr. Barnes' Band. (7) St. Paul's and Ardwick Green It dastrial Band (81 All Saints' Mission. (0) St. Ann's and Walker's Band. (10) Holy Trinity and Walton Band. (11) St. Luke's.

(12) Factory Yard lission. (13) St. Barnabas' and Preston Brook Band.

(14) St. James's and Volunteer Band. (13) Emmanuel Reformed Church. (16) Buckley-street. _ _

(17) Salvation Army and Band. (18) Ragged School and Drum and Fife Band.

It was indeed pleasing to see the long line of happy children with beaming faces, and it was fully evident that Walking Day had lost none of its charms for them. Each school had its handsome large banner with ribbons carried by gaily-attired girls; and bannerettes galore added to the attractiveness of the display. While there were not many attempts at the original, one could not fail to notice several features of more than usual picturesqueness. In this respect, the palm must be awarded to St. Peter's school, a large contingent of girls looking exceedingly pretty in dresses which were embellished with garlands, while on their heads were pretty wreaths of flowers. A number of boys figured in sailor costume, and a lavishly-decorated mail cart was a conspicuous feature. Scholars of several schools carried baskets of flowers and beautiful bouquets. St. Luke's displayed several floral arches which considerably enhanced the brightness of the procession. St. Barnabas School was headed by the handsome new embroidered banner bearing the figure of St. Barnabas as centre- piece. St. Clement's Church Lads' Brigade, in charge of Captain Gandy, Captain Lapage, Lieutenants Coates and Cotton,looked very smart and marched with military precision. A number of gaily decorated poles sur- mounted with floral crowns were carried in Emmanuel procession, which was particularly pleasing. As usnal, the Salvation Army and Ragged School which pursue a noble work among the poorest classes, brought up the rear. The object of the latter institution was aptly set forth on a poster which read :—" Beautiful Warrington. The Warrington Ragged School and Mission workers are trying to make Warrington socially beautiful by helping and educating poor children." The streets were thickly lined with spectators and the procession, which took fifty-five minutes to leave the Park, was watched with great interest, not the slightest hitch occurring. In the afternoon the youngsters betook themselves to fields where they disported themselves to their hearts' content. Games were indulged in with zest, those children who acquitted themselves best in the sp6rts returning home the fortunate possessors of prizes. .

- As in previon3 years, most of the Nonconformist schools gave their children a complete day's enjoyment in the country. Wycliffe scholars journeyed to Dunham Hill ; Gplborne-street Baptists to Lymm ; Bold-street Wesleyans to Lymm ; Workingmen's Mission and Sankey Bridges Wesleyans to Burtonwood; Cairo-street Unitarians to Walton Old Hall; and Friars Green and Brick-street Methodists to Padgate.

===== 0002601_19070629_art0151.txt | included=true | included =====
ROIAN C kTHOLTC PROCESSION.

Anyone with a genius for mathematics could estimate with a fair amount of accuracy the amount of hard cash expended in preparing the Roman Catholic children for the annual procession, but great as that total must be it counts for little against the display of self-sacrifice on the part of the parents involved in the pageant. Yesterday's procession, in the opinion of most if not of all who beheld it surpassed any of previ- ous years. It was a matter for considerable regret that it could be seen in its it entirety in Bridge-street only and it would be well if in the future the promoters would extend the route to include Sankey-street, Parker-street and Wilson Patten-street after the various schools have met at Bridge ',loot. This would mean that more time would be required but the difliculty could be met by starting a little earli3r. Yesterday there was far too long a wait after the ordinary procession had passed. As in the past the procession contained many features of special interest. The denomination is fortunate in possessing a number of magnificent banners and statues and these were displayed to the best possible advantage. The statue of the Blessed Virgin from St. Arary's, Butternaarket-street, in particular was worthily decorated with lilies. A special feature was the uni- formity displayed in the dressing of the children, and in the case of the little girls from St. Mary's, Latchford, who wore white dresses and blue sashes and veils sur- mounted by floral crowns,the effect was unusuall pretty. The general effect of the procession was charming in the extreme and in the constant outbursts of applause in which the onlookers were led to indulge, those who had faced the task of preparing the children,reaped their reward.

COUNTY PALATINE OF' CHESTER. NOTICE IS HEREBY GIVEN, that the next GENERAL QUARTER SESSIONS of the Peace for the COUNTY PALATINE OF CHESTER will be holden at the CASTLE OF CHESTER, in and for the said County on MONDAY, the Ist day of July, 1907, at Half-past Eleven o'clock in the Forenoon, when the Grand Jury will be empannelled. The hearing of all Appeals arising within the Petty Sessional Divisions of Broxton, Chester Castle, Dares- bury, Eddisbury, Nantwich, Runcorn, Winsford , and Wirral, forming the Chester District, and the trials of all prisoners committed from the last-mentioned Divisions, will take place at such Sessions. The Grand and Petty Juries for the Chester District must be in attendance at the Castle of Chester, at the Opening of the Court on MONDAY, the Ist of July, 1907, at Half- past Eleven o'clock in the Forenoon. THE COURT will be adjourned to the COURT HOUSE, KNUTSFORD, on WEDNESDAY, the 3rd day of July, 1907, at Eleven o'clock in the Forenoon. The hearing of all Appeals arising within the Petty Sessional Divisions of Altrincharn, Bucklow, Dukinfield, Middlewich, Sandbach, Congleton, Northwich, Prost- bury, and Stockport, forming the Knutsford District, and the trials of all prisoners committed from such Divisions will take place at such Adjourned Sessions. The Grand and Petty Juries for the Knutsford Pistrict must be in attendance at the Court House, I•:nutsford, at, the Opening of the Court on WEDNESDAY, the 3rd day of July next, at Eleven o'clock in the Forenoon. REGINALD porrs, Clerk of the Peace for the County of Chester. Chester, 12th June, 1907.
